--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This is the title page of the Russian translation of Karl von Eckartshausen's "Die Seiten-Zahlen aus dem Buch der Natur," known in Russian as "The Key to the Mysteries of Nature." This edition was published in St. Petersburg in 1804, a period when Eckartshausen's mystical and theosophical ideas were highly influential in Russian intellectual and Masonic circles.</meta>

# THE KEY

### TO

## THE MYSTERIES OF NATURE,

### a work by

## *MR. ECKARTSHAUSEN.*

<note>Karl von Eckartshausen (1752–1803) was a German mystic and philosopher whose works on Christian theosophy and the "inner church" had a profound impact on European occultism.</note>

---

### PART I.

<note>The following describes the allegorical engraving on the title page: A bearded male figure with a symbolic mark on his forehead and rays of light emanating from his head—representing wisdom or illumination—rises from a musical lyre. He holds a globe (the earth) in his right hand and a personified sun in his left, both tethered to the lyre by chains, symbolizing the harmonious connection between the celestial and terrestrial worlds. The lyre rests on seven books or tablets, likely representing the seven liberal arts or the seven stages of alchemical transformation. The entire structure sits on a pedestal inscribed with "THE LYRE OF ZABARTALAM" <note>original: "LIRA ZABARTALAM"</note>, a cryptic name frequently associated with mystical harmony in Eckartshausen's symbolic system.</note>

->St. Petersburg, 1804.<-

<summary>This is the title page of the 1804 Russian edition of Karl von Eckartshausen's influential mystical work, "The Key to the Mysteries of Nature." The page features a complex allegorical engraving symbolizing the divine harmony between the physical world and spiritual truths.</summary>
<keywords>Karl von Eckartshausen, mysticism, alchemy, Christian theosophy, St. Petersburg, 19th-century esotericism, Lyre of Zabartalam, symbolism</keywords>

# THE KEY

### TO THE

## MYSTERIES OF NATURE, <note>The term "Natura" in this context refers to the natural world viewed through the lens of natural philosophy—a blend of early science, theology, and mysticism popular in the late 18th and early 19th centuries.</note>

->*(Second Edition.)*<-

->The year 1820.<-

While the previous page was dated 1820, this text references earlier editions from the 1790s, suggesting this is a reprint or a continuation of a long-standing project.</meta>

## To the Reader.

We are fulfilling our promise to continue publishing the works of this dear Author. This book ought to have been the first to appear before you, kind Reader, as it was the first by which we came to know the Author, and as it was written before those we have already published: for its first volume was printed in its <term>second edition</term> <note>original: "вторымъ тисненіемъ" — literally "second pressing." This indicates the book was already popular enough to warrant multiple print runs by the late 18th century.</note> as early as 1791; while the fourth came out in 1792. But to undertake from the start the publication of a book consisting of several parts, before the Author had yet become known to the public, would have been uncertain: and other circumstances, moreover, did not permit it.

The <term>Writer</term> <note>original: "Сочинитель" — a common 18th-century term for an author or composer of a text.</note> says that his intention in writing this book was to make people more attentive to <term>Nature</term> <note>original: "Натурѣ" — capitalized in the original to denote the natural world as a grand, organized system, a common Enlightenment-era perspective.</note>, and to prove as much as possible that one should not believe everything, but neither should one reject everything; showing partly from theoretical observations and partly from practical experiments, that much that is wondrous and unbelievable is worthy of impartial 

<vocab>Author, Reader, Nature, 1791, 1792, Writer</vocab>

<summary>In this preface, the publisher explains why this specific volume is being released now, noting that while it was written and first printed in the early 1790s, they waited until the author was famous to commit to the full series. The author's goal is described as encouraging a balanced, skeptical, yet observant approach to the wonders of the natural world.</summary>

<keywords>preface, publishing history, Enlightenment, Nature, skepticism, 1791, scientific observation</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the publisher's introduction from page V. The previous page ended mid-word, describing "The illustration" on the title page. This page explains the mystical symbolism of that frontispiece, which depicts a divine figure with various allegorical attributes.</meta>

illustration on the title page, which we have retained from the Author, presents a symbolic image discovered in the year 1556 in Ethiopia <note>original: "Эѳіопіи" — In the 18th century, "Ethiopia" often served as a legendary or mystical setting for the discovery of ancient Hermetic secrets.</note>, bearing the inscription: <term>Lika Zarabshalam</term> <note>original: "Лика Зарабшаламъ"</note>: which signifies the <term>Creator of Worlds</term> <note>original: "Творецъ міровъ"</note>. The Author explains this figure in the following manner:

The head of the Elder represents the Creator of worlds, who created all things—the <term>Unity</term> <note>original: "Единица" — here referring to the Monad, the single source from which all existence originates.</note>. The three flames surrounding his head are a Symbol of perfection: in the corporeal world, they signify length, breadth, and depth; in the spiritual world, thought, intellect, and soul; in relation to bodies, number, measure, and weight; in relation to the soul, reason, memory, and will. The two hands, emerging from a single head, represent the first number that issued from unity—the number of creation—a symbol of manifestation. In the hands, the Sun and the Earth are bound together by a chain. The body with outstretched arms is a sign of accord, or Harmony. The books upon which it rests are the seven books of the <term>Mysteries of Nature</term> <note>original: "таинства Натуры"</note>, sealed with seven seals. The four strings on the instrument are the sign of the <term>tetrachord</term> <note>original: "тетрахордона" — a musical scale of four notes used in ancient Greece, often used in mystical texts to represent the harmony of the universe.</note> (four-stringed system), upon which the first musical concert was devised; it is also a symbol of regularity, for they signify the point, the line,

<summary>This page explains the complex occult symbolism of the book's frontispiece, interpreting a divine figure as a representation of Unity and cosmic harmony. It breaks down various elements—flames, hands, and strings—into their spiritual and physical meanings.</summary>

# THE KEY
## TO THE MYSTERIES OF NATURE.

### I.
### THE FIRST RULES

->For those wishing to dedicate themselves to the mysteries of Nature.<-

Only he whose heart is kind is worthy of knowing the mysteries of Nature; for he will employ his knowledge for the benefit of humanity.

Wisdom <note>original: "Премудрость" — a term often signifying Divine Wisdom or Sophia in mystical and theosophical traditions</note> is like the Sun, shining upon all people and warming all mortals: but, in order to feel the benefit of its light and warmth, the body requires a constitution <note>original: "организація" — used here to describe the physical and spiritual makeup or "tuning" of a person</note> that is in harmony with that light and warmth.

An evil man is unworthy of knowing the paths by which Science <note>original: "Наука" — in this context, it refers to "Sacred Science" or the study of the hidden laws of the universe</note> leads to bliss; for that which in Nature is a blessing for humanity, would in his hands become a curse <note>original: "клятвою" — in archaic Russian, this refers to a malediction or anathema rather than a promise</note>.

Fragrant flowers give off their scent in vain for the one who has no sense of smell, and the crumbs from the Lord’s table

### 2.

->ON THE HIDDEN MYSTERIES IN <unclear>NATURE</unclear><-
->AND ON THE METHOD OF FINDING THEM.<-

No man can <unclear>perfectly</unclear> teach another the <unclear>mysteries</unclear>. Nature herself is the priestess in <unclear>her own</unclear> temple, and she leads <unclear>into</unclear> the sanctuary of it <unclear>the one</unclear> who seeks <unclear>her</unclear> and who is <unclear>worthy</unclear>. He who has learned any mysteries <unclear>can</unclear> point the way to <unclear>them</unclear> to another who desires to <unclear>know</unclear> the mysteries, but the student must walk the path himself.

<term>Nature</term> (Натура) — in this context, Nature is treated as a sentient, divine force or a "Priestess" that guards hidden knowledge.
<term>Wise Man</term> (Мудраго) — refers to the ideal philosopher or "Sage" who possesses the discernment to find truth.
<term>Mysteries</term> (таинства) — the hidden laws of the universe, often related to alchemy or spiritual transformation.

<meta>This page continues the instructional discourse on the relationship between a teacher and a student in the pursuit of natural mysteries. It is not the Teacher's fault <note>original: "Не Учителева то вина"</note> if the student is lame, or crooked-legged, and limps where he ought to walk straight; or if he is cross-eyed and blind, and stares upwards when beautiful things are right under his feet; or if he runs headlong and stumbles when the Teacher <note>original: "Учитель"</note> commands him to walk at a steady pace.

Do not hurry! The greatest mysteries of Nature <term>Nature</term> <note>original: "Натура" — here personified as a divine teacher or a system of laws</note> are close to us, and simplicity finds them sooner than arrogance. Not everyone is worthy of the honor of being admitted into the Temple of Nature; the Divinity has so wisely arranged everything that the wicked cannot deceive Him.

The bird lives in the air, the fish in the water, man on the earth; each element <term>element</term> <note>original: "стихія" (stikhia) — the classical physical realms</note> requires a specific organization: so too the Temple of Mysteries <note>original: "Храмъ таинствъ"</note> requires a specific organization, proper to that sphere in which the Philosopher <term>Philosopher</term> <note>original: "Мудролюбецъ" — a literal Russian translation of the Greek "philosophos," meaning "one who loves wisdom"</note> wishes to dwell. He who desires to grasp the mysteries of Nature must study her great book. This book is Nature herself. Happy is he who knows the letters in which this book is written; happier still is he who can join them together; but most blessed of all is he who knows how to read this book.

Read this book, read with the eyes of the soul: and these eyes are observation.

The eye of your soul must be pure; you will achieve this purity through the quieting of passions.

Only in a pure spring is the image of the sun visible; its light is murky in a stormy river, where silt and mud stir up the water at the bottom. The same is true regarding Wisdom <term>Wisdom</term> <note>original: "Премудрость" (Premudrost) — In this context, it refers to Divine Wisdom or Sophia, a central concept in mystical and Masonic thought representing the hidden knowledge of God and Nature.</note>.

Wax melts from fire, wood burns: one must be stronger than wax and wood so as not to melt and not to burn. Personal discernment <term>discernment</term> <note>original: "разсужденіе" (razsuzhdenie) — The faculty of critical thinking and spiritual judgment.</note> is the primary necessity. He who reasons for himself is far higher than he who judges based on the opinions of others. One must think for oneself, examine for oneself, seek for oneself, and not gape at what others have found.

It is good to read; but to meditate even better. Whoever wishes to read, let him read with composure and detachment <note>original: "равнодушіемъ" — Here meaning spiritual dispassion or equanimity, rather than a lack of interest.</note>; cleanse your soul from prejudices toward the opinions of others; be gentle, and reject nothing simply because you do not know it; beware above all of pride.

### 3.
### On the Supernatural and the Miraculous

The mediating force of the active Deity <term>Deity</term> <note>original: "Божества" (Bozhestva) — A term often used by Enlightenment-era mystics and Deists to describe the Divine Principle.</note> in this corporeal world is Nature. Whoever thinks to separate the Deity from Nature takes away the soul from the body. Everywhere where God acts through mediation, the force of this mediated action of His is Nature.

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous section, moving from the definition of Nature as a divine tool to a philosophical explanation of how God, Nature, and human will interact through a chain of causality.</meta>

Weak human reason often calls "supernatural" or "preternatural" <note>original: "чрезъестественнымъ, сверхъестественнымъ" (chrez-yestestvennym, sverkh-yestestvennym) — The author uses two similar terms to emphasize things that seem to exist outside the normal laws of the physical world.</note> that which it cannot explain in Nature; and the supernatural consists more in the limitation of our understanding than in the essence of the thing itself.

In Nature, there are **Forces**, **Actions**, and **Effects**: everything consists of them. Forces and actions are not bound by necessity; their necessity is only *relative*: but effects are necessary; for effects are the offspring of actions, and actions are the children of forces. By this proposition, the Providence of the Eternal One and the freedom of human will are explained; for every force has countless actions, and every action has countless effects. Force resides in the *whole* <note>original: "всецѣломъ" (vsetselom) — Likely referring to the "Universal Whole" or the totality of the divine system.</note>, every action in force, every effect in action: everything together constitutes one chain.

Without God, Nature is a dead being. Nature is the herald of the Eternal; the organ proclaiming the Creator and connecting the Deity with the corporeal world. Within her is found the active force of the Deity for the good of the corporeal world:

<vocab>Nature, Forces, Actions, Effects, Providence, Eternal One, Creator, Deity</vocab>

<summary>This page argues that the "supernatural" is simply a limit of human reason and presents a causal chain of Forces, Actions, and Effects that allows for both Divine Providence and human free will.</summary>
<keywords>supernatural, causality, Providence, free will, Divine Force, Nature, Enlightenment mysticism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical exploration of Nature as a divine instrument. ...the active force of the Deity toward the blessing of the spiritual world is above Nature, and is the force of *Similitude of Being* <note>original: "подобобытія" (podobobytiya) — A philosophical/theological term suggesting a state of being that reflects or participates in the likeness of the Divine. It implies that the spiritual world operates on a principle of resonance with God.</note>. In it is contained the foundation of constancy and immortality.

Every exception from the ordinary circle of action is also based on Nature; she does not change, but the variety of actions is the cause of various consequences. Different consequences can have the same force as their cause, but not the same action. Man, being by nature extremely lazy, is always satisfied with the most immediate causes when explaining the phenomena he encounters. That is why in former times they attributed everything unusual to sorcery, while in the present, they deny everything they do not understand. And so the vice of former times was to believe everything; and that of the present—to reject everything they do not understand; for the wisdom of the majority of Scholars <note>original: "Ученыхъ" (Uchenykh) — Referring to the academic or "learned" class of the Enlightenment era.</note> is based on the opinions and stories of others.

There is a great difference between thinking and saying what others have thought and said, and thinking and saying from oneself.

In our age, Scholars have forgotten that theory owes its existence to practice, and that Nature existed before rules <note>The text cuts off at "прав-", likely "правилъ" (rules), suggesting that natural reality precedes the human systems used to describe it.</note>.

### 4.

## ON THE INCLINATION TOWARD THE MIRACULOUS AND SUPERNATURAL.

A spirit <note>original: "Духъ" (dukh) — in this context, it refers to the mind, soul, or the internal disposition of a person.</note> incapable of appreciating the true and the elegant is more easily attracted by the miraculous and the strange. The more ignorant and foolish a person is, the more quickly their imagination is set in motion. For a lazy spirit, the most pleasant food is miracles and curiosities; everything extraordinary holds a double charm for the ignorant. For the ignorant, even the most ordinary things are extraordinary.

The more wondrous and strange fables <note>original: "басень" (basen) — here used to mean myths, tall tales, or superstitious stories rather than just animal fables.</note> a people possesses, the more foolish, superstitious, and consequently ignorant that people is.

It is the duty of the industrious and the wise to penetrate into the connection of occurrences <note>original: "приключеній" (priklyucheniy) — historically, this referred to "happenings" or "events" in nature rather than the modern sense of "adventures."</note> and phenomena, for which reflection is required; but the lazy man does not reflect, and allows himself all manner of reveries, provided they entertain his imagination.

The infant and the savage <note>A typical 18th-century comparison between early childhood and "primitive" societies, both seen as driven by imagination over reason.</note> are satisfied with any answer, so long as it tickles their fantasy in some way. The most pleasant thing for a person is that which touches them in a special way, and—

<vocab>spirit, miraculous, imagination, superstition, ignorant person, fantasy, occurrence</vocab>

<summary>This page argues that an obsession with the miraculous and supernatural stems from intellectual laziness and ignorance, suggesting that a wise person seeks the rational connections between events instead of being led by fantasy.</summary>
<keywords>superstition, imagination, ignorance, reason, fables, nature of the soul</keywords>

...brings his imagination into a pleasant agitation <note>original: "потрясеніе" (potryaseniye) — literally "shaking" or "shuddering," here meaning a state of being emotionally or mentally moved.</note>. From this comes the savage’s love for the miraculous, the inclination of unenlightened nations toward superstition <term>superstition (суевѣрію): literally "vain faith," referring to irrational beliefs in the supernatural</term>, trust in charlatans <term>charlatans (шарлатанамъ): fraudulent people claiming to have special knowledge or skills</term>, a thirst for stories about ghosts <note>original: "привидѣніяхъ" (privideniyakh) — apparitions or spirits</note>, and the tendency of the female sex toward fortune-telling <note>original: "ворожбѣ" (vorozhbe) — sorcery or divination</note> and card reading.

A person would rather be deceived by expectations <note>original: "чаяніями" (chayaniiami) — hopes or anticipations</note> than seek out the truth <note>original: "подлинности" (podlinnosti) — authenticity or genuineness</note>. Little by little, he learns of various goods; a desire is born in his soul to enjoy them: but since this requires labor and—what is even more tedious—reflection, and he prefers only to desire, whatever promises him success in his desire with the least effort is thus more pleasant to him.

From this it follows that a charlatan is more respected in the world than a wise man; that he more quickly finds patronage among the rich and noble; for the rich and noble are, for the most part, lazy, and would wish to obtain everything without further effort.

From the knowledge <note>original: "познанія" (poznaniya) — here meaning the study or understanding of human nature</note> of man, the following rules emerge:

The more enlightened <note>original: "просвѣщеннѣе" (prosveshchenneye) — a central concept of the Age of Enlightenment, referring to the development of reason and education</note> the mind of a person or a nation becomes, the more...

The more a Scholar <term>Scholar (Ученый): in this context, someone who relies on formal, book-learned knowledge rather than direct experience or original thought</term> speaks from books, the less he possesses an original mind; the more light and truth spread, the weaker the strain on the imagination becomes—and the more the miraculous disappears.

->5.<-

## OBSTACLES ON THE PATH TO THE MYSTERIES OF NATURE.

Only the Wise Man finds the mysteries <term>mysteries (таинства): referring to the hidden laws or sacred truths of the natural world</term> of Nature. While testing the paths prescribed by others, he walks with his own step.

Nature is like a friend; she has no secrets from those worthy of her friendship and company. Curiosity, pride, vanity, blind partiality to the opinions of others, and scholarly arrogance are will-o'-the-wisps <note>original: "блудящіе огни" (bludyashchiye ogni) — literally "wandering fires," or ignis fatuus; deceptive marsh lights that lead travelers astray</note> on the path to the mysteries of Nature. We often think ourselves close to them when we are furthest away.

<vocab>Nature, Wise Man, mysteries, truth, Scholar, imagination</vocab>

<summary>This page argues that true wisdom requires an original mind rather than book-learning and identifies moral failings like pride and vanity as the primary obstacles to understanding the secrets of Nature.</summary>
<keywords>Nature, wisdom, scholarly arrogance, mysteries of nature, imagination vs. truth, will-o'-the-wisps</keywords>

--- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of human understanding versus the hidden depths of the natural world. It transitions from the previous page's discussion of original thought into a critique of the current states of science and philosophy, using the metaphor of Nature as a modest but enticing woman to explain why truths remain hidden.</meta>

Why do you have eyes? Why ears? Why hands? Is it not so that you yourself might see, yourself hear, and yourself touch? Why then do you wish to see with the eyes of others, hear with the ears of others, and touch with the hands of others? Whoever understands this principle will be in a position to explain many phenomena in Nature <term>Nature (Натура): here personified as a living, semi-divine force governing the material and spiritual world, rather than just the physical environment</term>.

Our knowledge in Physics <term>Physics (Физика): in this era, this referred to "Natural Philosophy"—the study of the physical world and its laws, which the author considers to be in a primitive state</term> is still mere toys in comparison with those things hidden from us; Nature sometimes communicates one of her secrets to man only to entice him into a relationship with her. She is like a beauty who sometimes reveals her minor charms, but diligently hides the primary ones.

Our Philosophy is still in its infancy: and what was called philosophy before was barbarism <note>original: "варварство" (varvarstvo) — likely referring to the Scholastic or medieval traditions that the author believes lacked true spiritual or scientific insight</note>.

<margin>p</margin>
In the Temple of Nature there have been many great Priests <note>original: "Жрецовъ" (zhretsov) — those initiated into the deeper mysteries; the term suggests that true science is a form of sacred service</note> who preached: but the people did not understand them, and their writings have been turned into ridicule by the ignorant.

If one wishes to translate from one language to another, one must be skillful in both. — While a man

<margin><unclear>vertical pencil mark</unclear></margin>In the vast Physical shops <term>Physical shops (Физическіе магазины): likely referring to "Cabinets of Physics" or laboratories filled with scientific instruments, which were popular attractions for the curious in the 18th century</term> there are also philosophical toys; Learned boys amuse themselves with them, and kind mother Nature laughs, watching their schoolboy games.

Do not neglect the knowledge of the human body. ## 7.

# CHAPTER NECESSARY FOR RESPONDING TO THE PRECEDING QUESTIONS.

Unity is a great mystery of Nature <term>Nature (Натура): here personified as a divine, living system</term>, the goal of all things. The closer any being approaches toward unity, the more perfect it becomes—a proposition that not everyone will understand.

Everything living in Nature feels within itself an attraction to this; everything strives toward it on the wheel of time, only some reach it sooner, others later.

The great law of this goal is love; it is the active force, the means to similitude of being <term>similitude of being (подобобытіе): a specialized term suggesting the process of becoming like the Divine or reaching a state of essential harmony with the source of existence</term>, and the chain of unity. It is the commandment of the Deity, impressed upon the hearts of all men, and it is the knot binding together all beings. For this, the Wise Man <term>the Wise Man (Мудрый): a reference to the enlightened seeker or the "Sage" common in late 18th-century philosophical texts</term> gives thanks: for the power of spirit over spirit, for the hidden powers of the soul, and for the magnetic force. —

### 9.

# THINGS OF WHICH FEW HAVE A CONCEPTION.

Concerning that which is outside the circle of our sensory perception <term>sensory perception (чувственность): the capacity to perceive the world through the physical senses</term>, we have no conception. Every person is limited in their concepts by the level on the ladder of creation upon which they stand. Therefore, if someone claims that they understand words spoken to them about things entirely foreign to them, one may rightfully doubt it. For this reason, I too, speaking of things of which few have a conception, have reason to doubt that I will be directly understood.

<margin>?</margin>

<margin>p.</margin>
Man currently understands everything through the senses; the senses, however, are limited and subject to deception, and consequently, our concepts are also limited and deceptive. And as we rarely judge a thing by itself, but rather by our concepts of it, this is the source of our errors. The soul, however, being independent of the body, looks into the thing itself, and consequently knows the thing along with its actions and its effects. Its vision is not a scrutiny <note>original: "разглядываніе" — a slow, piece-by-piece examination</note>, but a single instantaneous glance, in which power, action, and effect constitute a single whole.

Coarse organs <term>coarse organs (грубые органы): the physical sensory apparatus such as the eyes, ears, and skin</term> are needed only for the manifestation of the inner power of life in creatures <term>creatures (твари): all created beings in the natural world</term>, but not at all <gloss>not</gloss> for their existence. From this comes the conclusion: that which is produced by this power does not depend on coarse organization and can stand on its own.

### 10.

### TO SEEK FOR ONESELF IS NOT THE SAME AS FOLLOWING OTHERS.

There are things very close to us which, however, are unknown to the majority of people, partly because they do not know the organ <term>organ (органъ): in mystical literature, this refers to a "spiritual sense" or faculty of perception, such as the "eye of the heart," rather than a physical body part</term> through which these things are perceived; and partly because the nature of these things is such that people cannot find this organ, even if they knew of it. Everything has a necessary cause <note>The author is likely referring to the principle of sufficient reason: that everything must have a reason or cause for being as it is</note> by which a thing is what it is: with a change of these circumstances, the thing ceases to be what it was and begins to be something else.

## II.

### ON THE LANGUAGE HAVING NO WORDS.

There is a language which has 
no words, but is more excellent than all 
languages. Thoughts are born 
in us from impressions produced 
by objects upon our senses. 
To receive these impressions is 
called *to understand*; to sort 
through them within oneself is called *to* 
*think*; to renew these concepts is 
called *to remember*; and to explain 
them with words is *to speak*.

Every creature <term>creature (тварь): in this historical context, "тварь" refers to any created being or thing in the natural world, not just animals.</term> explains itself 
and consequently *speaks*; and its language 
corresponds to the perfection of the creature. 
Beasts utter sounds and change [them]...

The variety of impressions produced

--- Page 54 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical exploration of language, moving from the general concept of signs to a specific distinction between the intuitive "language of the heart" and the verbal "language of the reason."</meta>

There are two languages: the language of the heart, and the language of the reason. The language of the heart is less subject to deception than the language of the reason. The language of the heart draws its images from feelings, while the language of the reason draws from words *about* feelings. The language of the heart has few words, yet speaks much; whereas the language of the reason has many words, and often says very little. The more complex a language is, the more imperfect it is; for it is thus more subject to delusions <note>original: "заблужденіямъ" — can mean errors, delusions, or wanderings of the mind.</note>.

The hieroglyphs of the ancients were a contemplative language, or a language of the eye. Among the Chinese, there are as many letters <note>The author likely refers to logograms or characters here, rather than an alphabet.</note> as there are words.

The ear is the weakest and most deceptive sense: yet it is through it that we learn almost everything. Why should we wonder then that humans are subject to so many delusions? There is only one direct language in Nature <note>The author uses the capitalized "Натура" to personify the natural world as a source of truth.</note>—contemplation—the language of the soul.

Show a Frenchman, a German, an Italian, an Englishman, or an Arab a rose; each will understand the same thing. But if you speak the word "rose" to them, they will not understand it in the same way. And so, it is words that are the cause of our not understanding one another, and not the things themselves.

The language of the passions has its own co- <note>The text cuts off here mid-word; likely "сообразность" (consistency/suitability) or "совершенство" (perfection).</note>

<summary>The author distinguishes between the intuitive "language of the heart" and the verbal "language of the reason," arguing that words and hearing are primary sources of human error and misunderstanding.</summary>

...mirror-like quality in the human eye. Whoever is accustomed to looking into this mirror of the soul will understand more from a single glance than from a thousand words; for words are always only words. Harmoniously attuned souls receive identical impressions: and so there is a means to *know the thoughts of another.*

Spirit does not converse with spirit; for their thoughts are already a conversation. Their language is *contemplation* <note>original: "созерцаніе" — in this context, it refers to direct, intuitive perception of truth without the need for verbal reasoning.</note>, and contemplation is the knowledge of the thing itself—truth—the avoidance of error—the language of the soul.

There are people who understand this language: but they are few; because if this language were to become more common, then given our current coarse actions, it would be harmful: everything ascends toward perfection gradually.

"Meaning locked within the letter," says Herder <note>Johann Gottfried von Herder (1744–1803), a German philosopher who argued that language is the vessel of human thought and culture.</note>, "struggles to break free from it: our best thoughts perish in dead letters."

"When we perceive all things at once with the mind, and not just their separate attributes; when we utter the very nature of things, rather than some arbitrary signs of them: then error and opinion..."

### 12.

## **On Reality in Obscure**
## **Sensations.**

Everything is *reality*, or *authenticity*; imagination itself is authenticity, only in a lesser degree.

In this bodily world, man receives his knowledge through the senses. Each sense is capable of receiving impressions, and through these impressions, images of things are communicated to our soul. These impressions remain for a longer or shorter duration, depending on the property of the organ or the impression itself.

Man feels even before he knows that he is feeling. Such sensations are *obscure sensations* <note>original: "ощущенія темныя" — referring to "dark" or "dim" perceptions that occur below the level of conscious awareness, a concept common in 18th-century psychology.</note>.

<summary>The text argues that spiritual paths must be discovered individually through one's own 'Genius' and begins a psychological inquiry into how sensory impressions form the basis of human knowledge, even before they are consciously recognized.</summary>
<keywords>Truth, contemplator, Genius, reality, authenticity, imagination, senses, soul, obscure sensations, sensory impressions</keywords>

--- Page 57 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous page regarding "obscure sensations" (subconscious perceptions). It provides a practical anecdote to illustrate how the mind records information without conscious awareness, which then resurfaces during sleep.</meta>

...by them many incomprehensible things are explained. Presentiments, premonitions, visions, and sympathetic actions are based on the *theory of obscure sensations* <note>original: "теоріи темныхъ ощущеній" — This refers to the psychological concept, popularized by philosophers like Leibniz and Wolff, that the soul perceives many things "obscurely" or "darkly" below the threshold of conscious awareness.</note>.

I knew a man who, while carrying an important letter to the post office, dropped it along the way and was extremely distressed by this loss. At night, he had a dream: as if some man, in a gray caftan <note>original: "кафтанѣ" — a traditional long-sleeved Russian coat.</note>, on such-and-such a street, picked up his packet and entered a nearby tavern <note>original: "трактиръ" — a historical term for a public inn or restaurant.</note> with it. In the morning, he went to that tavern and indeed found that very same man there, in that very same caftan, and received his lost letter from him.

This incident is not difficult to explain. When he was walking to the post office, his thoughts were occupied with something else, and not with the letter. While walking, he dropped it and saw right there the man who picked it up and went with it into that tavern. He saw all of this, but not clearly—obscurely—because he was thinking about something else entirely. At night, when he grew still from those thoughts, that idea was renewed and became clear within him, and in his sleep, he remembered it all.

<summary>The author uses a story about a lost letter to explain the 'theory of obscure sensations,' showing how the subconscious mind records details that the conscious mind ignores, only to reveal them later in dreams.</summary>
<keywords>obscure sensations, subconscious, dreams, premonitions, psychology, memory</keywords>

### On Reality in Obscure Sensations.

Everything is *reality*, or *authenticity*; even imagination is authenticity, only to a lesser degree.

In this corporeal world, man receives his knowledge through the senses. Each sense is capable of receiving impressions, and through these impressions, the images of things are communicated to our soul. These impressions remain for a longer or shorter duration, depending on the nature of the organ or the impression itself.

A person feels even before he knows that he is feeling. Such sensations are *obscure sensations* <note>original: "ощущенія темныя" — also known as "perceptions without awareness," a key concept in pre-Freudian psychology regarding the subconscious mind.</note>,

Nothing, not even the smallest thing, happens

is as if it were imprinted <note>The Russian word *впечатлѣніе* (impression) share the same root as "to print" or "to stamp," suggesting a physical mark left on the body.</note> in our **fibers** <term>fibers: (Russian: *волокна* or *фибры*) In 18th-century physiology, "fibers" were thought to be the fundamental structural units of the body and nerves that vibrated or reacted to external stimuli.</term>.

With every impression upon the senses,
our nerves and fibers are shaken and
brought into a certain **passive state** <note>original: "страдательное положеніе" — a philosophical term meaning a state of being acted upon by an external force, rather than initiating an action.</note>.
Imagination can renew

object which was initially required
to produce this state; and
therefore imagination is a relative
authenticity—that is, through the imagination of an
absent object, my nerves and
fibers are brought again into the same
state in which they actually
were when the object first
touched them through the senses. Thus
we recall to memory the image of
some person, the scent of a flower, the voice—

->### FURTHER RULES.<-

Every state of the soul presupposes a certain indispensable disposition of the fibers and nerves <note>In 18th-century physiology, "fibers" and "nerves" were often likened to the strings of a musical instrument; their tension or "tone" determined a person's emotional and physical state.</note>, without which that state of the soul either cannot exist, or would not be as it is.

The disposition of the nerves and fibers depends on the temperament; the temperament on the humors <note>original: *соковъ* (literally "juices"). In the medical theory of the time, "humors" referred to bodily fluids like blood and bile that governed health and personality.</note> and the circulation of the blood; and this, in turn, on the diversity of the air, food, and so forth.

Thus, he who knows how to change these things also knows how to attune the nerves in a person and bring the soul into a known state. Upon this is founded the knowledge of the actions of spirit upon spirit, and of soul upon soul. — The reflecting mind will understand this.

Several years ago, it happened that I was in the company of a decent man who, in conversation, complained that his Superior <term>Superior: *Начальникъ*—a term referring to a high-ranking official or commanding officer in the Russian civil or military service.</term> could not tolerate him. I performed another similar experiment with a man who could not tolerate roasted piglet. Having reached an agreement with one of his best friends, we went to visit him, taking with us a piece of roasted piglet in a pocket. From the first glance at us, he appeared dissatisfied, and finally even confessed to his friend that he himself did not know why, but that his friend was unbearable to him today. On the next day, we came to him again, but now without the piglet, and he was very glad to see us.

## 14.

### The Theory of the Imaginative Power with an Explanation of Certain Phenomena.

Knowledge of a present object is sensation: but there is another faculty within us that produces the same feeling in the absence of the object. It is *Imagination*.

*Imagination* can be *voluntary* or *involuntary*. The first is independent of the mechanism of the organs and their agitation, which is produced in them by circulating <term>humors: *соки*—the vital fluids or "juices" of the body in early medical theory</term>; the second consists of a specific disposition of the organs and a strong agitation of the <term>fibers: *фибры*—considered at the time to be the basic structural threads of the nerves and muscles</term> through the blood.

And so, whatever sets the blood in motion can also set the imagination in motion, as we see examples of this in diseases <note>The author is likely referring to fever-induced hallucinations or delirium</note>.

A quiet and calm circulation of the blood produces quiet and calm imaginings; a rapid and turbulent one, fearsome and terrifying ones; a difficult circulation of the blo- <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely continuing with "blood" (крови) on the next page.</note>

**Theory of imaginative power, Imagination, voluntary, involuntary, mechanism of organs, fibers, blood, diseases**

<summary>This page defines imagination as the ability to perceive an object in its absence and distinguishes between voluntary imagination and involuntary imagination caused by physical states like blood flow and the agitation of bodily "fibers." It suggests a physiological basis for mental states, linking the movement of blood to the nature of a person's visions or thoughts.</summary>
<keywords>imagination, physiology, voluntary vs involuntary, blood circulation, fibers, humors, medical philosophy</keywords>

fear, timidity, and anguish are the consequences of stagnant blood circulation. The strength or weakness of these effects depends on the <term>temperament: the ancient medical theory that human personality and health were governed by the balance of four bodily fluids</term>. Highly agitated blood in **Sanguines** produces frivolity <note>original: "ветренность" (vetrennost), meaning flightiness or lack of focus</note> and recklessness; in **Cholerics**, it produces rage and madness. Stagnant circulation of the blood produces very poor consequences in **Phlegmatics** and **Melancholics**.

The circulation of the blood and <term>humors: *соки* (soki)—literally "juices," referring to the vital fluids of the body</term> depends largely on food, on the air which we swallow <note>i.e., breathe in</note>, and on the elasticity of the parts of our body.

The imagination can be acted upon through all five senses: through food, aromatic vapors <note>original: "куренія" (kureniya), referring to incense or medicinal fumigations</note>, sounds, touch, and the presentation of objects to the sight, which bring the fantasy into involuntary motion. Memory is the companion of imagination. Like images excite similar ones within us. Our concepts, whether they depend on our will or not, are either simple and singular, or composite and complex: which arise from the same causes as the simple ones.

<meta>This page moves from the theoretical principles of the soul and imagination discussed on the previous page to a practical demonstration of how the imagination can be manipulated through suggestion and physical tricks, followed by the introduction of chemical influences like incense.</meta>

Once at a ball, I was asked by a young man in love to show him his beloved <note>original: "любовницу" (lyubovnitsu). In the 18th and early 19th centuries, this term simply meant a sweetheart or ladylove, without the modern implication of an illicit affair.</note>. I knew her, and I had with me various **miniature portraits** <term>miniature portraits (миніатюрные портретцы): small, hand-painted likenesses often kept in lockets or pockets, used here as a prop for a "magical" illusion.</term>, from which I chose one that resembled her. Then I ordered a glass of water to be brought to me and went with it into another room, asking him to recall his beloved to his memory as vividly as he possibly could. Meanwhile, I used wax to stick the portrait I had chosen to my palm and deliberately clouded the water so that it would not reflect in it too quickly or too clearly. "Sit down," I told the lover, "in my place; cross your arms as I do, crosswise; press them to your forehead, and look intently into the water." As soon as the water grew still and the portrait began to reflect in it from my palm, it seemed to his **heated imagination** <note>original: "разгоряченному воображенію" (razgoryachennomu voobrazheniyu). A common phrase in early psychology to describe a mind over-excited by passion, making it prone to hallucinations.</note> that he truly saw his beloved: so much so that this man often came to me afterward and asked for the same thing, and by this action, I provided him with several pleasant hours.

But nothing acts so powerfully upon the imagination as **fumigations** <term>fumigations (куренія): the practice of burning incense, resins, or herbs to create smoke. This was believed to have a direct physiological effect on the "spirits" of the brain.</term>. One canno—

In the 18th and 19th centuries, this was the standard term for a person of Jewish faith/descent, though here it refers to the recurring literary trope of the "Wise Oriental" or "Arabian Kabbalist" common in mystical tales.</note>, who had lived for a long time in Arabia, had taught him the secret of summoning the deceased and the absent by means of **fumigation** <term>fumigation (куреніе): the ritual burning of incense or herbs. In this context, the smoke was often used as a screen onto which the mind could project images, or it contained substances meant to induce a trance.</term>. 

I began to plead with him to perform this experiment in my presence. 

— "You know," he replied, "that I am no charlatan, and I do not make a trade of this craft; therefore, you may be certain that I will not deceive you. I shall prove this by revealing the secret itself to you. You will see something wondrous," he continued, "the nature of which I myself do not know, and I make no judgment on whether it is worthy or unworthy; I assert only that it is no trick; and if it is not exactly 'real,' it is certainly not mere imagination either. You shall see this for yourself."

After a certain **preparation** <note>original: "пріуготовленіи" (priugotovlenii). This likely refers to a period of fasting, prayer, or sensory deprivation intended to make the subject more susceptible to hallucinations.</note>, to which I subjected myself and which I shall describe below, a day and hour were set for the experiment. I came to him and named the person whom I wished to see. He threw into the vessel standing before...

...that without this **preparation** <note>original: "пріуготовленіе" (priugotovleniye). The preparation, handed to me by the **Scotsman** in writing, was of the following content:

> I promised you, my dear Sir, to show you a deceased or absent person. I wish to keep my word to you: but promise me also that you will sacredly fulfill what I shall prescribe for you, however unimportant it may seem to you.
>
> The reason for my requirement lies in the very nature of the matter. Perhaps there are such relations between things that, if you fail to fulfill even a single point, physical harm to your health or well-being will follow, and it will have a grievous effect upon your **moral state** <note>original: "нравственное состояніе" (nravstvennoye sostoyaniye). In the 18th century, this term encompassed one’s spiritual, psychological, and ethical health.</note> throughout the rest of your life. Therefore, promise me to fulfill exactly what I prescribe for you, as faithfully as you truly desire your own well-being.
>
> 1. For eight days, abstain from all strong **passions** <term>passions (страсти): In the philosophical language of the time, "passions" referred to any intense emotions or physical urges—such as anger, lust, or greed—that were believed to disturb the soul's equilibrium and cloud the intellect.</term>.

<summary>The narrator receives written instructions from the Scotsman detailing the necessary spiritual and physical preparations for summoning an apparition, including a stern warning about the potential psychological and physical dangers of the ritual.</summary>

2. In the course of these eight days, drink little.

3. Do not meet with many people.

2. What is their gender?

3. Was it kinship, friendship, or love that united you?

4. What was their **temperament**? What did they look like? Who were they? Where did they live? <note>In the 18th and 19th centuries, "temperament" referred to the Four Humors (sanguine, choleric, melancholic, phlegmatic), which were believed to dictate a person's character and appearance.</note>

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion of occult experiments. ### *Extract from a letter, written from*
### *V. * * on December 17, 1785.*

--- And so there truly are such things in **Nature** <note>original: "Натурѣ" (Nature). The author uses the archaic Russian "Natura," personifying the natural world as a divine system of laws and secrets.</note>, of which our **philosophy** does not even imagine. <note>This is a paraphrase of Hamlet’s famous line: "There are more things in heaven and earth, Horatio, than are dreamt of in your philosophy."</note> The **Divinity** has hidden much from mortals, and the **Eternal** has sealed many of Nature’s **mysteries** <note>original: "таинства" (tainstva). This term carries a double meaning of "mysteries" and "sacraments," suggesting that natural secrets are sacred.</note> from us with an unbreakable seal. 

Not all is fantasy; much, perhaps, is the very truth: for remember, my dear friend, that once immeasurable seas separated humans from one another; **Europeans** did not know of **Americans**. Perhaps there are similar **partitions** <note>original: "средостѣнія" (sredosteniya). Literally "mediastinum" or "dividing walls," here referring to the metaphysical barriers between the human world and the world of spirits.</note> located between humans and other beings, of which many mortals until now are unaware, just as they were once unaware of the **Americans**. — Much may be deception, much may be forgery: but surely not all. 

<term>Swedenborg (Сведенборгъ)</term> <note>Emanuel Swedenborg (1688–1772) was a renowned Swedish scientist and theologian who claimed to converse with spirits and angels. He was a major influence on 18th-century occultism.</note> and <term>Falk (Фалькъ)</term> <note>Likely Samuel Jacob Hayyim Falk (c. 1710–1782), known as the "Baal Shem of London," a famous Kabbalist and alchemist whom contemporaries often viewed with a mix of awe and suspicion.</note> were certainly not scoundrels, and yet their lives are incomprehensible to us; and perhaps for many they will remain incomprehensible until the **grapes ripen**, and the time comes to press the wine from them. Man invented the ship, stepped onto a new path, and became acquainted with the unknown—

<summary>An extract from a 1785 letter arguing that mystical phenomena are as real as the then-newly discovered American continent, citing figures like Swedenborg and Falk as legitimate mystics rather than frauds.</summary>
<keywords>Nature, philosophy, mysteries, Emanuel Swedenborg, Samuel Falk, mysticism, 18th-century letters</keywords>

--- Page 94 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's observations on the relationship between physical health, diet, and the quality of dreams, following the recipe for a lemon balm vinegar mentioned at the end of page 61.</meta>

has nothing harmful in it; as a result, one sees beautiful places in their sleep. I have experienced this myself. But for this to work, it is also necessary that the soul not be troubled by any strong passions, that the stomach not be overly burdened with heavy foods, and the blood not be overheated by wine. 

One acquaintance of mine was always plagued by terrible dreams, so much so that he went to bed in fear every night. I advised him to soak his feet in warm water for several days in a row and gave him **saltpeter** <term>saltpeter: "селитра" (selitra), or potassium nitrate; in older medical practices, it was sometimes used as a cooling agent or sedative to "calm the blood"</term> in lemonade. Before bed, I instructed him to chew **lemon balm** <note>original: "мелисса" (melissa)</note> and smell the vinegar prepared from it. In a short time, his disordered **imagination** was restored to order, and his dreams became peaceful and pleasant.

The consumption of beans, lentils, and peas, especially in the evening, produces heavy dreams.

### 15.
## Chapter on the Soul.

The being within us that feels, thinks, wills, and acts is the soul.

The body itself does not feel, does not will, and does not act: thus, the being that feels, acts, and wills is distinct from the body.

No created spirit <note>original: "духъ" (dukh); here referring to a finite, created intellect (like a human or angel) as opposed to the Divine.</note> can penetrate into the inner depths of its nature; happy is he to whom it reveals even its outward veil. <note>This is a poetic expression of Enlightenment-era skepticism: the "essence" of the soul is considered unknowable; we can only observe its outward manifestations or "veil."</note>

The soul is not **composite**, not **corporeal**, and is **indivisible**. <term>composite: "сложна" (slozhna). In 18th-century philosophy, if something was "composite," it was made of parts and could break; because the soul is "simple" (not composite), it was argued to be immortal because it cannot be broken down.</term>

The soul possesses the power to feel, to think, and to act. It does not always feel, think, or act; but the possibility of feeling, thinking, and acting is always found within it.

---

ments and machines, about which Guyot, Funk, Martius, and Halle <note>These were well-known 18th-century authors (Edme-Gilles Guyot, Christlieb Benedict Funk, Johann Nikolaus Martius, and Johann Samuel Halle) who wrote about "natural magic" and the use of physics to create illusions.</note> have written so much, and of which we have seen examples in part in the **phantasmagoria** <term>phantasmagoria: A form of horror theater that used "magic lanterns" to project frightening images, such as ghosts or skeletons, onto smoke or semi-transparent screens.</term> of M. Robertson <note>Étienne-Gaspard Robert (known as Robertson) was a famous Belgian physicist and stage magician known for his "Fantasmagorie" shows in the late 1790s.</note>: but as this does not properly belong to the subject of this Book, we omit them, proceeding directly to the matter following this. *Translator’s Note.*

The transition from the soul to the body is a transition from light to twilight: the transition from the body to the soul is a transition from twilight to light. Nature shows us this truth in the way she presents objects to the eye, and how the eye perceives them.

The line extending from the eye to an object increases: a point becomes a line, and the line gradually expands into the physical magnitude of the body. <note>The author is describing the "cone of vision," where a single point of perception in the mind expands to encompass a large physical object.</note>

A dead being is something impossible in nature—a fiction. Every destruction is a transition to a higher life.

Every creature has its own life; only the forces of life act differently; and this depends on the constituent parts of the body and the manner of their composition. The question: "Will man exist after death?" is the same as asking: "Will an artist still be an artist when he no longer has his tools, and when the statue he carved crumbles into dust?" The artist arrived, and with his creative brush, he summoned the image from the body of the paints; thus, the image entered the kingdom of paintings, independent of the coarse mass of the pigments. —

Is it not possible, says **Iselin** <note>Isaak Iselin (1728–1782) was a Swiss philosopher of the Enlightenment; his work *History of Mankind* often touched on the spiritual and moral development of the human race.</note>, for these beings to act upon us in a hidden manner by means of the **imagination**? **Spirit** acts upon spirit, like reflected light upon objects. Man communicates his thoughts to man through sounds, or tones. An accepted and defined modification of these tones produced **words**. And so, it is possible both to see and to hear directly, through the movements of the **fine organization** <note>original: "tonkaya organizatsiya" — the subtle nervous or spiritual structure of a person, as opposed to the physical body</note>, without the mediation of the **coarser organization** <note>The physical body and its external sense organs like the eyes and ears</note>. The thought of **higher beings** <note>Celestial or spiritual entities</note> surpasses human thought in purity and, through the guidance of the soul, enters clearly into the finest organization of the thinker. It becomes a human thought; yet it remains pure, like a sunbeam gilding the mountain peaks at bright noon.

A human being, when speaking, produces tones or sounds which are formed into words, enter the ear, and reach the waiting soul. But higher creatures do not speak with us in this way. The tone of their voice

Such omens of approach exist in every kind: for this, only delicate organs are required.

## ON VISIONS HAVING THEIR FOUNDATION UPON THE DECEPTION OF THE SENSES.

We have spoken of visions based on soulful sensation and the <note>The author uses "finest organization" to refer to a person's refined nervous and spiritual makeup that allows for supernatural perception.</note> finest organization: now let us speak of visions based on the deception of the senses.

Among these I place various phenomena explained by the rules of Optics and <term>Catoptrics|The branch of optics dealing with the reflection of light, specifically by mirrors.</term>. To these I attribute the wondrous appearances in the dark of figures resembling humans; the vision of one’s own self <note>This refers to autoscopy, the experience in which an individual perceives their own body from an external perspective.</note>; and the artificial appearance of living persons according to the rules of Optics.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on optical deceptions introduced on page 98, specifically focusing on how atmospheric conditions like smoke and fog can create the appearance of phantom figures.</meta>

Regarding the first of these, it must be noted that smoke and fog are capable of reflecting light and can present the shadows of images. In the autumn, during the evening, fog hovers over rivers, marshes, ponds, and damp places. Approaching such a place—especially when walking eastward while the setting sun illuminates from behind—it often happens that the shadow of the walking person is reflected in the fog; it then appears as if a <term>black peasant|Russian: *chernyi muzhik*. In 19th-century Russian, this term refers to a dark, shadowy silhouette or a common spectral figure appearing in folk tales.</term> is walking directly toward you.

This phenomenon can also be seen in a room, if it is filled with enough smoke for it to swirl in columns. If several people enter this room and the last one carries a candle, then the shadows of those in front will be reflected in the smoke, making it appear as if several black peasants are standing in the room. This phenomenon will vanish as soon as the first person who entered takes the candle, for the cause of the shadow’s reflection will naturally disappear.

Water vapors form a mirrored surface; and if there is a dark body behind, one can see oneself in them. <note>The author is describing the physical basis for certain types of "ghostly" apparitions, explaining them as simple reflections in vapor rather than supernatural entities.</note>

<note>original: "черный мужикъ" (black peasant/dark figure), "отраженіе свѣта" (reflection of light), "водяные пары" (water vapors)</note>

<summary>The author provides scientific explanations for ghostly sightings, illustrating how shadows reflected in fog or smoke can create the illusion of dark, approaching figures. It often happens that chests filled with clothing, after standing for a long time in a cellar or in a damp place, accumulate such vapors within themselves. If someone should happen to open them unexpectedly, it may occur that they see themselves through the reflection of these vapors. The image vanishes instantly because the air entering upon the opening of the chest disperses the vapors and, consequently, destroys the mirror-like surface. In dense forests where there are marshes and damp places, the same may occur, especially on a clear and calm day.

A reliable man <note>original: "достовѣрный человѣкъ" (a trustworthy or credible person)</note> once told me that upon opening a chest of clothing, he saw his own image; he was so terrified by this that he fell unconscious. It is likely that this occurred in the manner described above.

### 18.

## VISIONS WHICH HAVE THEIR BASIS IN BODILY ILLNESSES AND IN THE APPROACH OF DEATH.

Man in this corporeal world always depends on Nature. In vain does the proud man deem himself a distinct being <note>The author suggests that humans are not separate from the natural world, but part of its machinery</note>. The Elements which support his life act upon both his spirit and his body, in both healthy and sickly states.

It is only necessary, says Pernetti <note>Antoine-Joseph Pernety (1716–1796), a French writer known for works on hermetic philosophy and natural history</note>, to turn one's eyes toward the objects closest to us to be convinced that everything surrounding, nourishing, and growing our body constantly acts upon it, and can produce great changes in the soul, with which it is so closely bound.

It has been stated above how great the power of the imagination is over man; what manner of fantasies can be produced by corrupted bile, thickened blood, and the halted circulation of humors <term>humors: refers to "соковъ" (juices), the ancient medical theory that health is governed by the balance of bodily fluids</term> is testified to by illnesses themselves.

<note>original: "Видѣнія, болѣзни, Натура, Стихіи, Пернетти, воображеніе, желчь, кровь, обращеніе соковъ"</note>
**Visions, illnesses, Nature, Elements, Pernetti, imagination, bile, blood, circulation of humors**

<summary>The author transitions from discussing optical illusions to exploring how physical ailments and the natural elements influence the human spirit and create internal visions. He cites Pernetti to argue that the soul is deeply affected by the body's biological state, particularly through the "humors."</summary>

--- Page 136 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the physiological causes of hallucinations, moving from general environmental influences to the specific mechanical failures of the body's "fibers" and nerves.</meta>

Thus, there are visions that have their basis solely in illnesses. They arise from fatigue, or the straining of the nerves.

There are also such states in which only certain fibers <term>fibers: in 18th-century physiology, "fibers" (фибры) were thought to be the basic structural units of the body, which vibrated to transmit sensory information</term> of the finest organization can be set in motion, while all others are incapable of vibration <note>original: потрясенію — here referring to the physiological agitation or oscillation of the nerves</note>. From such a state arise unconsciousness, dullness, and stupidity.

There are, again, states in which a single fiber is constantly strained and no longer contracts as it should according to its elastic property. From this arises madness, in which a person is always occupied with a single thought.

I cannot better explain this state by way of comparison than by likening it to an organ <note>the musical instrument</note>. As soon as the springs that blow air into the pipes lose their elasticity, you will hear no tone at all; but if one spring is overstrained, then a single tone will be heard continuously: whereby, consequently, all harmony is destroyed.

<note>original: "видѣнія, болѣзни, нервы, фибры, потрясеніе, безпамятство, сумасшествіе, гармонія, упругость"</note>

--- Page 137 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the physiological basis of mental states, specifically expanding on the organ metaphor from the previous page where "strained" or "slack" fibers determine the nature of a person's condition.</meta>

This latter state can very easily occur in children and people with a delicate nervous constitution, especially during a moment of great fright.

Anything that overstrains the nerves—every powerful passion—can, according to the nature of one’s physical constitution, produce some degree of madness <note>original: сумасшествія</note>.

The proud and self-willed are particularly prone to this; for their stubbornness is already evidence of a natural inclination toward raving madness <note>original: сумасбродству — in this context, a state of wild or irrational extravagance</note>.

Kind and gentle characters are rarely subject to raving madness; instead, they are more likely to fall into stupidity or a state of frenzy <note>original: безуміе</note>, which differs from insanity in this: raving madness is based on excessively strained nerves, while stupidity stems from nerves that have become too slack <note>original: опустившіяся — literally "dropped," implying a loss of necessary tension or "tone"</note>.

Therefore, the stupid or the half-witted <term>half-witted: полоумные, a term used historically for those with diminished mental capacity or cognitive "dullness"</term> do not remember much; for within them, one idea suppresses another, and not a single one is properly formed. Their condition is pitiful, and consequently, doctors make a great mistake when they treat the half-witted in the same manner as they do the insane.

When, during an illness, people of weak vision become sharp-sighted, and those hard of hearing begin to hear with ease, this is a certain sign of death. This change is the consequence of an immeasurable tension, the cause of which lies in the final effort of a struggling nature <note>original: натуры — here referring to the biological life force or physical constitution</note>.

There are people who recognize the hour of their own death. This occurs due to the capacity of the soul, which knows the measure of its own bodily powers.

However, this also requires a particular organization <note>original: организація — in the historical sense, the structural arrangement and functional state of the body's organs</note> that is not common to everyone. One who understands the chain and the spring of a watch can easily determine the time when it will run down. Anyone is capable of determining, by sight alone, when the sand will flow out of an hourglass by measuring the time by the fallen sand; in just the same way, people, through an internal sense, feel the exhaustion of their machine <term>machine: машина, a common Enlightenment-era metaphor for the human body as a mechanical or hydraulic system</term>, and thus can determine their own passing.

It is asserted that people often see themselves <note>This likely refers to the phenomenon of autoscopy or seeing a "double" (doppelgänger), which was historically associated with the approach of death</note> before death, and I do not reject this in any way. The corrupted vapors <note>original: изпаренія — historical term for "effluvia" or subtle fluids thought to be emitted by the body</note> are ill-suited for the same circulation <note>original: обращенію — the flow or movement of these substances</note> they possessed in the body’s healthy state; they lose their volatility, becoming coarser and heavier, more difficult to separate from the body, and more prone to clumping together. They become more visible, and therefore, upon the slightest reflection, an image of the person appears within their own vapors, as if upon a mirror-like surface, which was mentioned above.

Such phenomena may often be a natural omen of a dangerous illness or impending death. Their foundation lies in nature <note>original: натурѣ — referring to the biological constitution or physical world</note>, in the very essence of our body and the properties of its constituent parts.

There is also a circulation of our vapors in the air, which pertains to the preservation of the animal body <term>animal body: животнаго тѣла, referring to the biological organism and its vital functions</term>.

Many diseases arise from the corruption of these vapors in the air; yet physicians treat the body, instead of striving to correct the atmosphere <note>original: атмосферу — here referring to the immediate envelope of air and emissions surrounding an individual</note> that surrounds it.

### 19.

## ON PREDICTIONS AND PROPHECIES.

To predict means to determine future occurrences before they actually take place.

Human beings measure time by the impressions that objects leave upon the senses; from this arise the past, present, and future, which are based on the changing states of our perceptions.

Our senses are limited: the understanding of things, which enters the soul through our most delicate organization <note>original: тончайшей организаціи — likely referring to the nervous system or the biological structure of the sense organs</note>, proceeds gradually. We feel that we cannot contain different concepts within ourselves all at once, but only one idea after another.

Time and distance are attributes of the physical world; for they are forms of change to which the spiritual world is not subject.

In the spiritual world, all that is possible is always present.

So long as the human spirit languishes in its bodily bonds, it perceives things only sequentially: its gaze be-

<vocab>Predictions, Prophecies, Time, Spiritual world, Physical world</vocab>

<summary>The author defines prediction and argues that while humans perceive time linearly due to biological limitations, the spiritual realm exists in an eternal present where time and distance do not apply.</summary>
<keywords>predictions, prophecies, nature of time, spiritual vs physical world, sensory perception, human spirit</keywords>

--- Page 141 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical argument from page 108, concluding the sentence regarding the spirit's perception as it detaches from the physical body.</meta>

comes more perfect when it is freed from these
bonds.

If we were to rise high above our terrestrial globe, we would always see the sun: but here it does not always remain for us; and this transience <note>original: преходимость — the quality of being temporary or passing away</note> of it, which constitutes our day, lies not in the essence of the sun, but in the nature of the earth revolving around the sun: from which come the rising and setting of the sun, midday and midnight. The sun is transient only relatively, and not in its own essence.

Eternity has neither time, nor space, nor beginning, nor end. Therefore, the Pre-eternal One <note>original: Превѣчный — a theological title for God, signifying existence before and beyond time</note> sees all the future by His very nature, for to Him everything is the present.

The limitation of our senses prevents us from seeing the future; for our feelings are based on impressions within the organs, which, being only gradually excited, have limits.

Man judges the future only by comparing the present with the past; he is permitted to know the future

Is it not possible, according to the physical laws <term>physical laws</term> <note>original: физическіе законы — in this context, referring to the observable rules of the material world as understood by Enlightenment-era science.</note> accepted until now, to find a way to accurately predict the future?

I pondered this question for a long time; at last, I arrived at this: I shall give you a sealed note <term>note</term> <note>original: записочка — literally a "little note" or slip of paper, often used in the context of sealed letters or parlor demonstrations of foresight.</note>; keep it wherever you wish, and open it at the appointed time: you will then find written within it that which, perhaps, no person could have known at the time the note was written. ### 21.

## On Sleep and Drowsiness, and the Condition of Somnambulists
## and Hysterics.

Our concepts in this corporeal world depend upon the senses; they convey objects to the soul.

A person whose senses are not dulled can always see, smell, touch, and hear.

It happens sometimes that he sees, smells, hears, and touches at one and the same time: but such an action of objects upon all the senses at once does not provide a clear perception, but produces a dim sensation <term>dim sensation</term> <note>original: темное ощущеніе — in the philosophy of the era, this refers to a confused or "obscure" perception where the mind is aware of an external stimulus but cannot distinguish its specific qualities.</note>.

<vocab>Nature, sleep, drowsiness, Somnambulists <note>original: Лунатики — literally "lunatics," but in this historical medical context, it refers to sleepwalkers influenced by the cycles of the moon.</note>, Hysterics, senses, soul, sensation, grace</vocab>

<summary>The author concludes his philosophical invitation by emphasizing that the "secret" of his method is accessible through hard work rather than mere curiosity, before beginning a new inquiry into how the soul perceives the world through the senses and the nature of altered states like sleep.</summary>
<keywords>Nature, labor, divine grace, senses, soul, perception, somnambulism, hysteria, Enlightenment psychology</keywords>

--- Page 152 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from Section 21 regarding the relationship between the soul and the senses. It transitions from the definition of "dim sensations" to the mechanics of focus and the physiological effects of sensory overload or balance, leading into a specific historical medical reference.</meta>

The more the soul occupies itself with a single object, the more the organs of that sense through which this object reaches the soul are activated. For example: while listening attentively to some story, we become, so to speak, "all ear," seeing and feeling nothing; in the same way, sometimes we become "all eye."

When, however, all our senses are suddenly set in motion by many objects at once: then we are, as it were, stunned.

If such a shock <note>original: потрясеніе — a term used here to describe the impact or vibration caused by external stimuli on the nervous system.</note>, produced by objects upon the sensory organs, is strong: then the soul falls into confusion.

If, however, it is mild: then from it there arises in us a kind of pleasant stunning. The soul feels something inexplicable, which sometimes casts us into such a pleasant drowsiness <term>drowsiness</term> <note>original: усыпленіе — a state of light slumber or being lulled into a trance-like state.</note> that it cannot be explained.

It was into this very state that the famous Doctor in London, Graham <note>original: Греэмъ — James Graham (1745–1794) was a Scottish physician known for his "Temple of Health" and the use of "celestial" electricity and magnetism to treat patients.</note>, sought to bring people; this state is nothing other than the restored equilibrium of the sen- <note>The text ends mid-word: "чув-" (чувствъ), meaning "senses."</note>

<summary>This page explains how concentrated attention on one sense can "blind" the others and how balanced, mild stimulation of all senses can lead to a pleasant, trance-like state of equilibrium, referencing the techniques of Dr. James Graham.</summary>
<keywords>soul, sensory perception, attention, stunning, drowsiness, James Graham, equilibrium</keywords>

sory sensations, or a balanced relaxation of all sensory organs.
When all the senses are brought into a state of equal harmony: then this state strengthens them, and they receive new strength and activity.
The merciful Deity has granted us this beneficial state in sleep. Nature <term>Nature</term> <note>original: Натура — personified here as a guiding physical force, common in Enlightenment-era natural philosophy.</note> then raises gentle, irrigating vapors to the head, and moistens the strained fibers <term>fibers</term> <note>original: фибры — the fundamental threads of the body and nerves, believed to vibrate or tighten with sensation.</note> with them. From this irrigation, they lose some of their usual tension; they relax a little, and from that, the person sinks into sleep.
As the humors <note>original: соки — literally "juices," referring to the bodily fluids or "four humors" believed to govern health and temperament.</note> are in the body, so is the sleep. Thick, sticky fluids produce a deep and heavy sleep: thin and pure ones produce light and pleasant sleep.
For this reason, drinks <note>original: напитки — likely referring to alcohol or "strong spirits" which were thought to "dry" the nerves.</note> prevent one from sleeping peacefully; for they dry out the nerves and fibers, and deprive them of the gentle moisture with which they are irrigated during sleep.
Just as the dew, falling from the sky in the evening, moistens the flowers and gives rest to their vessels, so that with the coming morning they may again be capable of na- <note>The text ends mid-word: "на-", likely leading to "напряженію" (tension) or "начинанію" (activity).</note>
