--- Page 154 ---
<meta>This page completes the sentence from page 121, continuing the physiological explanation of sleep through the lens of 18th-century natural philosophy. It contrasts deep sleep with the state of drowsiness (dremota) using poetic metaphors and anatomical theories of that era.</meta>

tension: thus beneficial Nature <term>Nature</term> <note>original: Натура — personified here as a guiding physical force, common in Enlightenment-era natural philosophy.</note> in man raises thin humors <note>original: соки — literally "juices," referring to the bodily fluids or "four humors" believed to govern health and temperament.</note> into the brain, irrigates the nerves and fibers <term>fibers</term> <note>original: фибры — the fundamental threads of the body and nerves, believed to vibrate or tighten with sensation.</note> with them, and gives them relaxation, in order to bring them back into a state to act once more.

What dew is for plants, sleep is for man in the economy <term>economy</term> <note>original: экономіи — referring to the "animal economy," a historical term for the internal management and functional balance of a living body.</note> of this bodily world.

Drowsiness differs from sleep by the degree of tension in the nerves.

In drowsiness, objects still act upon the senses: but as the soul rests and does not properly direct any sense, it does not retain the impressions <note>original: впечатлѣнія — literally "impressions," the physical marks or "stamps" that external objects were thought to leave on the sensory organs.</note> produced in them.

A light zephyr <note>original: зефиръ — a gentle west wind, often used in classical literature to represent a soft, cooling breeze.</note> in spring sways only the little flowers, and a small boat glides along the ripples of a smooth lake: so is falling asleep sweet to weary limbs.

How pleasant it is to drowse in a garden, when a quiet breeze lulls the senses, when the sound of a murmuring brook echoes in the ears, and the fragrance of flowers touches the sense of smell! —

I see, hear, smell, and taste only when something touches me and sets in motion the nerves of the organ of sight, hearing, smell, or taste; in other words, when I feel by touch. To touch with the eyes is called to see; to touch with the ear, to hear; to touch with the nose, to smell; to touch with the tongue and palate, to taste.

A change in the particles touching my organ changes sight, hearing, smell, and taste.

Particles contrary to our organization excite unpleasant impressions <note>original: впечатлѣнія — in Enlightenment philosophy, these were viewed as physical "stamps" or "marks" left by external objects upon the soft matter of the sensory organs.</note> in our senses; while those harmonizing with us excite pleasant ones.

22.

### PROPOSITIONS NECESSARY FOR THE EXPLANATION OF SOMNAMBULISM.

There is in Nature a liquid substance, common to all things, which the Ancients called *first matter* <note>original: "первою матеріею" (pervoyu materiyeyu), referring to the alchemical concept of *prima materia*, the formless base of all matter.</note>; and modern philosophers call the *universal fluid* <note>original: "fluide universel." This was a central concept in Mesmerism (animal magnetism), suggesting an invisible physical medium through which life-force and physical forces travel.</note>.

All that is bodily is contained within this fluid.

Its modifications are countless.

It is the cause of forms, and the foundation of a thousand changes.

Within it lies a productive force; it is magnetism, and electricity, and the matter of heat, and light, and so forth.

The elements <note>original: "стихіи" (stikhii), referring to the four classical elements—earth, air, fire, and water—which were thought to be the first manifestations of physical reality.</note> are its first emanations. It is the organ of creation, the root of all things; the Deity produced it for the production of worlds.

The first modification of this most subtle substance was the creation of the elements.

The property of this universal fluid is purity and simplicity in the highest degree, as far as is possible for bodily things.

<vocab>somnambulism, first matter, universal fluid, magnetism, electricity, elements, creation</vocab>

<summary>The author defines the "universal fluid" (or first matter) as a primordial, God-given substance that manifests as electricity, magnetism, and light, serving as the fundamental building block of the physical universe. This substance is presented as the essential medium required to understand the mechanics of somnambulism.</summary>
<keywords>universal fluid, first matter, magnetism, electricity, somnambulism, natural philosophy, elements, creation</keywords>

It contains and forms everything. Enclosed in coarse bodies, it is the cause of the growth of metals, the vegetation of plants, and the preservation of animals. This describes the process by which the divine or primordial source flows into and fills the material world.</note> and in the filling of all bodies. 

Its property is action, inflow, and outflow <note>original: "втеченіе и изтеченіе" (vtechenie i iztechenie). In the context of 18th-century science and mysticism, this refers to the "influx" and "efflux" of universal fluids like animal magnetism.</note>.

Clothed in the most subtle body, it is called **Light**.

The action of this first matter upon its first emanations—those enclosed in the most subtle bodies—is motion, action, circulation, and life.

<meta>This page continues the list of manifestations of the "first matter," picking up directly from the end of the previous page which concluded with "and from this—"</meta>

produces motion, activity, circulation, growth, and life.

The action of the emanations <note>original: "изліяній" (izliyaniy), literally "pourings out." In this context, it refers to the way the primordial substance flows into and animates physical forms.</note> of this matter, and the action of the matter upon these emanations, varies according to the properties of the bodies containing these emanations.

In the most subtle bodies, such as in light, the motion of these emanations is exceedingly rapid.

The second degree, following *Light*, is the *Magnetic fluid* <note>original: "Магнитическое жидкое вещество" (Magniticheskoe zhidkoe veshchestvo). In the 18th century, "fluids" were invisible substances—like magnetism, electricity, or heat—that were thought to flow through the universe like water.</note>.

Being enclosed in particles that are not so subtle, these emanations are not set into such a subtle motion; and for this reason, they surround creation like a sea.

The third degree is the *Matter of heat*. It consists in the compression of numerous physical particles pregnant with the outflows <note>original: "изтеченіями" (iztecheniyami), meaning the "efflux" or streaming out of a substance from its source.</note> of light; and therefore it multiplies the internal action of this outflow in such a way that the emanations, by their very nature, strive to return to their source. This striving of theirs constitutes the friction of the particles, and the gentle destruction of these particles creates heat.

The fourth degree is the *electrical force*.

<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of the "first matter," transitioning from the general classification of the four degrees (Light, Magnetism, Heat, and Electricity) to the specific mechanical laws of resistance and force that govern them.</meta>

The emanations of this first matter <note>original: "первой матеріи" (*prima materia*), the fundamental primordial substance in alchemical and natural philosophy.</note> are of such a nature that they constantly act back, as we stated above, upon that mass from which they flow out.

This reciprocal action corresponds to the quality of the bodies in which these emanations are enclosed, and to the resistance of the physical particles.

If the resistance of these particles is not equal to the emanation, as occurs in light: then the first matter acts with its full force, just as in light; but if the resistance and the force are equal to one another, so that neither the particles of the body overcome the force of the inflows <note>original: "втеченій" (vtecheniy), the internal streaming of matter into a form.</note>, nor the force of the inflows overcomes the force of the physical particles of the body: then from this equality is born a fluid substance called *magnetic*.

It is like scales standing in equilibrium, in which at the slightest shift in weight, one pan outweighs the other.

If the striving of the struggling, enclosed forces of the outflows <note>original: "изтеченій" (iztecheniy), the efflux or streaming out from a source.</note> is stronger than the physical particles enclosing them: then the striving of these inflows back toward their source overpowers the resistance of the particles so that they are little by little liberated;

**Suns** are not burning bodies, but bodies of light—worlds of superior quality—which by their rapid rotation set into motion the atmospheric particles, and by this motion produce heat, **electric force**, and fire.

Magnetism has the same foundation, and differs from electric matter <term>Electric matter: 18th-century scientists viewed electricity as a "subtle fluid" or a specific type of matter that could flow through objects.</term> only in that here the light is locked within even finer particles, which therefore offer it less resistance. And so, the magnetic force does not rupture the bodily particles as the electric force does, but carries them away together with itself: from which occurs the attraction of the magnet.

Every body is filled with electric and magnetic force, with the only difference being that one is filled with them more, and another less.

### ESSENTIAL RULES FOR THE KNOWLEDGE OF ELECTRIC AND MAGNETIC FORCE.

1. Electric force is twofold: atmospheric and terrestrial.

<summary>The author explains how the absorption of "inflammable" particles through the pores and lungs causes disease by upsetting the balance of bodily humors. He proposes that electricity and magnetism are the primary tools for restoring health and introduces their different forms.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the numbered list of "essential rules" regarding electric and magnetic forces, specifically focusing on atmospheric electricity and its interaction with the human body through pores.</meta>

2. Atmospheric force is generated two or three hundred paces from the earth.

3. At noon, in calm weather and under a clear sky, the atmospheric electrical force is *positive*; but in the morning and evening, it is *negative*.

4. Water vapors repel it, while dew and fog attract it.

5. Atmospheric electrical force has a great influence on all bodies, especially on organized beings <note>Organized beings: A historical term for living organisms, which were thought to have a complex "organization" of vital forces.</note>.

6. Organized beings are the best electrometers <term>Electrometer: An instrument used to detect and measure electric charge.</term>, or measurers of this force.

7. According to Leeuwenhoek's <note>Antonie van Leeuwenhoek (1632–1723), a Dutch scientist and pioneer of microscopy.</note> observations, the sweat pores in human skin are so small that 250,000 of them can be covered by a single grain of sand.

8. <insert>/</insert> When ointments, liniments, and Spanish flies <term>Spanish flies: Also known as Cantharides; these were dried beetles used in traditional medicine to create blisters or as a topical stimulant.</term> penetrate into the blood through the skin: then how much more easily can electric and magnetic forces penetrate there?

9. <insert>(</insert> 9. The fissures, or pores, in the lungs and in the hair are the constant channels between atmospheric electrical force and the human body.

<summary>The author describes the properties of atmospheric electricity and argues that because the skin and lungs are filled with microscopic pores, the body is highly susceptible to electric and magnetic influences.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the numbered list of observations regarding the interaction between atmospheric electricity and the human body, following the discussion of skin pores and the lungs as conduits for these forces.</meta>

10. Every minute we breathe twenty times, and with each breath, we inhale approximately forty cubic inches of air.

11. The cavities, or bubbles <note>The author is referring to the alveoli, the microscopic air sacs in the lungs where gas exchange occurs.</note>, in the lungs hold at least 220 cubic inches.

12. When the atmosphere is in a negative state, the human body exhales through the lungs and the sweat pores as much of its excess as possible.

## ON THE MEDICAL EFFECTS OF ELECTRICAL FORCE.

The Chevalier Sousselier de la Tour <note>likely Claude-Adrien Sousselier de la Tour, a French researcher who, in the late 18th century, explored the "medical electrical" theories popular at the time.</note> loved to practice Medicine, believing that the science which brings healing and relief to man is the most important and useful. Being compassionate toward poverty and misfortune, he never missed an opportunity where his knowledge could be of use to people. Seeing, however, that the medicines he prescribed did not always produce the expected effect, he resolved to follow a different path. Observing that sharp objects have the property of diverting <term>electrical matter: In the 18th century, electricity was often described as a "subtle fluid" or "matter" that flowed through and around bodies.</term> to another place, he decided to see if he might find in this method what he had sought in vain among herbs and roots.

He made for himself two small discs out of cork-wood, and into each he inserted <note>The text ends here mid-sentence, likely leading into a description of a specific electrical device or experiment.</note>

<summary>This page concludes a list of biological electrical properties and introduces the work of Chevalier Sousselier de la Tour, an 18th-century practitioner who turned to electricity as a medical treatment after finding traditional herbal remedies insufficient.</summary>
<keywords>medical electricity, Chevalier Sousselier de la Tour, electrical matter, healing, animal electricity, cork discs</keywords>

twenty needles each; on one disc he magnetized them, but on the other he did not, and he covered the discs on the side where the needle heads were located with lead discs. Then he stood upon an *insulator* (*), bared his right knee, and applied the magnetized needles to it at a distance of 5 or 6 <term>lines: An old unit of measurement, roughly equal to 2.1 millimeters or 1/12th of an inch.</term>, connecting them to the <term>machine: Referring to an electrostatic friction generator, common in 18th-century laboratories.</term> by a small chain, which was also attached to the lead disc. After that, he took a chain in his left hand, which connected him to the <term>conductor: The part of the electrostatic generator that collects and holds the electrical charge.</term> of his machine, and ordered himself to be electrified. When the globe had been turned 60 times, he stepped off the insulator and saw that not only on the side where the needles had been applied, but also on the other, the entire knee had turned red; and he felt a gouty pain—which he had also felt slightly before—in the big toe of his left foot. After a minute the pain passed, and the red spot disappeared; he became healthy,

Pain in a hollow, decayed tooth, he says, occurs not because the nerve is exposed and the outside air enters therein, but from thickened humors <note>original: "мокроты" (mokroty) — in 18th-century medicine, this referred to bodily fluids or "phlegm" believed to cause illness when they became stagnant or thick.</note>; for, upon the cure of the illness, the tooth and the nerve remain in the same condition.

He electrified them in the following manner:—He placed the patient on an insulator <note>A platform or stool with glass legs used to prevent the electrical charge from escaping into the ground.</note>, connected him to the conductor <note>A metal rod or chain that transferred the static electricity from the friction machine to the patient.</note>, and held his small disk with needles—from which the electrical matter <note>At the time, electricity was often conceptualized as a physical fluid or "matter" that could flow into the body.</note> streamed—near the aching cheek, at a distance of six lines <term>line: A traditional unit of measurement; one line is approximately 2.1 millimeters or 1/12 of an inch.</term>, directly opposite that tooth. Many who suffered from tertian fever <term>tertian fever: A type of fever (often associated with malaria) where the paroxysms or spikes in temperature occur every third day.</term>, he cured in three days, making from 60 to 80 turns of the globe, depending on the age and constitution of the patient.

The best time for electrification is on non-fever days; three days are sufficient, and it is not necessary to be electrified more than twice.

# SOME NEW AND SPECIAL EXPERIMENTS
## CONCERNING ELECTRIC FORCE.

I made for myself a special kind of amalgam <note>original: "амальгаму" (amalgamu) — a mixture of mercury with another metal. In early electrical science, these were spread on the friction pads of electrostatic generators to greatly increase the production of "electric fluid."</note> from zinc and mercury.

Taking one part zinc and three parts mercury, I amalgamated them in the known manner, and added white lead <note>original: "бѣлилъ" (belil) — likely ceruse or white lead (basic lead carbonate), used here to give the mixture the necessary consistency.</note>, as much as was needed, so that it became a thickish mixture, into which I poured melted sheep tallow, and created a kind of ointment. Whenever I proceeded to electrification, I smeared the cushions <note>These cushions or pads pressed against the rotating glass to create the friction necessary for static electricity.</note> around the glass globe with this ointment; and the thinner the layer, the better the effect. The effect was very strong. After turning the globe a few times, I obtained an extraordinarily large spark; and this experiment can be performed in any weather.

From melted phosphorus, mixed with sheep tallow, mercury, and zinc, an excellent amalgam is also made.

If you place it in water and let it sit for several days, then, after gently pouring the water off, soak a sponge with this water: then one can obtain

<meta>This page outlines the theoretical principles of the experiments described on page 161, where the author claimed to share thoughts and sensations with a subject. It transitions from practical demonstrations to a philosophical-scientific explanation based on the laws of electricity as understood in the late 18th century.</meta>

Equally electrified bodies do not act upon one another; rather, there must be more of this force in one than in the other.

One can increase or decrease electrical force through art <note>original: "искусствомъ" — meaning through the practitioner's skill, technique, or the use of specific instruments.</note>: the former by *positive*, the latter by *negative* electrification.

Positive and negative bodies exist in a state of full electrical action with one another, provided the proportion of these bodies is equally correct.

Two bodies existing in full electrical action with one another are, in regard to their electrical connection, so to speak, one body in two parts.

When two bodies are brought into full electrical action: then no change can occur in one without a corresponding change following in the other.

Since a change in one body affects a change in the other: then a change in the sensations of one body must necessarily affect the sensations of the other; for sensations are nothing more than a change of state <note>This is the core theoretical justification for "mesmeric rapport": the idea that two people can become a single electrical circuit, allowing for the transfer of physical and mental feelings.</note>.

The parts of the body exist in harmony with the whole: from this, bodily sensation arises.

In the sensation of an electrical shock, a thousand people positioned in a chain <note>This refers to a popular 18th-century demonstration where a line of people held hands to conduct a charge from a Leyden jar, feeling the shock simultaneously.</note> constitute, as it were, a single person.

It is a pity that few will understand this language, and that it is impossible to speak clearly for those who have never reflected on this!

## Animal Magnetism.

That fluid substance which <term>Mesmer</term> <note>Franz Anton Mesmer (1734–1815), a German physician who developed the theory of "animal magnetism," later known as mesmerism. He believed an invisible natural force flowed through all living things.</note> called *Animal Magnetism*, is nothing other than the second degree of light in relation to the corporeal world, as we have stated above.

It is an extremely subtle <term>substance</term> <note>original: "существо" — used here in its archaic philosophical sense meaning "essence" or "fundamental matter" rather than a living creature.</note>, elastic, and capable of penetrating every body.

As it is the lightest of all fluid substances: it restores equilibrium in all things and prevents their confusion <note>In the 18th-century context, "confusion" refers to a lack of order or the breakdown of distinct forms.</note>, from which Chaos would again arise.

This fluid substance, a child of light, possesses all its mobility and is the cause of the motion of all things.

<summary>This page concludes a mystical reflection on the unity of beings and introduces the concept of "Animal Magnetism" as a subtle, light-like fluid that maintains universal equilibrium and motion.</summary>
<keywords>Animal Magnetism, Franz Mesmer, Sacrament of Unity, Nature, Chaos, subtle fluids, equilibrium</keywords>

--- Page 199 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical and metaphysical description of the "magnetic fluid" (Animal Magnetism) introduced on page 164, detailing its cycle between the ethereal and corporeal worlds.</meta>

Its throne is in the boundless space of creation. It takes its form according to the variety of bodies which it penetrates and surrounds. Being unitary in its origin and infinite in its modifications, it is the chain of all things.

In it lies the cause of the movement of all beings.

By it, the fluids <note>original: "соки" — literally "juices," referring to the internal humors or biological fluids believed to circulate within a living organism.</note> in the animal body are moved.

Mineral magnetism, combustibility—or <term>phlogiston</term> <note>In early modern chemistry, phlogiston was a substance supposed to be inherent in all combustible bodies and liberated by burning.</note>—and everything else are its effects.

Receiving motion from light, it flows incessantly from the higher <term>ethereal regions</term> <note>The "aether" was historically considered the fifth element, a subtle substance filling the space of the universe beyond the terrestrial sphere.</note> into the corporeal world.

Its influence upon the bodies here is pure, and the manner of its outflow is true magnetism.

Its return flow to the regions of light is no longer so pure and is more corporeal: having clothed itself in a physical covering, it becomes **electrical matter**, until it is again freed from these acquired parts and returns in purity to its source.

Electrical matter is the first modification of the magnetic fluid,

The purer the air, and the more it is cleansed of <term>combustibility</term> <note>original: "горючесть" — In the context of 18th-century natural philosophy, this refers to the property of being able to burn, often associated with the presence of "phlogiston," a hypothetical substance once thought to be contained within combustible bodies.</note>, the closer it is to magnetic matter.

The more <term>combustibility</term> there is in the air, the more prone it is to ignition, and the closer it is to **electrical matter**.

*(*) Above, the Author called the electrical force the fourth degree, but here, the third. From this, we notice only (as is also evident from all his writings) the fertility of the Author and the abundance of his knowledge, by which he, so to speak, did not "compose" but merely wrote, simply pouring his thoughts onto paper, and perhaps sometimes did not manage to read over what he had written. Without that, he would certainly have noticed this contradiction himself, given how close these passages are to one another; it arose only because he has now lost sight of the <term>matter of heat</term> <note>This refers to the "caloric theory," an obsolete scientific theory that heat consists of a self-repellent fluid called "caloric" that flows from hotter to colder bodies.</note>, which he had included previously. For this reason, we have judged it best in our translation to leave both these passages without our corrections, in their innocent negligence.*

The ordinary method of magnetizing is as follows:

The magnetizer <note>A practitioner of animal magnetism, also known as Mesmerism.</note> sits opposite the person whom they wish to magnetize, face to face; and in order to bring themselves into harmony with them, they place their hands upon the other's shoulders and move them down from the shoulders to the very tips of the fingers, touching the thumb of each of the patient’s hands for some time. This is repeated two or three times; then the magnetizing begins from head to toe. Here, through touch or questioning, they try to discover the seat of the illness, which for the most part is found on the opposite side; especially in cases of paralysis, rheumatism, and similar ailments. Having found this spot, they move their hands over it until the disease reaches a crisis <note>In the context of Mesmerism, a "crisis" was a physical reaction—often involving shaking, sweating, or sleep—that signaled the expulsion of the disease and the restoration of magnetic balance.</note>, which is the only means to a complete cure.

One touches with the thumb and index finger, or the palm, or even with a single finger, drawing a line across that entire area in the direction of the nerves, or even with all five fingers.

To treat illnesses with the help of trees, one must magnetize them in the following manner:

Choose a young, strong, branchy, straight tree without snags <note>original: "сучьевъ" — knots or protruding dead branches</note>, especially a thick oak, elm, or beech; and standing opposite it from the southern side, begin to trace with your finger, or an iron rod, or a small stick, from the leaves to the branches, having first drawn several such lines along the main branch. Thus does Halle <note>Johann Samuel Halle (1727–1810), a German professor whose writings popularized scientific and pseudo-scientific discoveries</note> describe the method of magnetizing. ### Page 189

At which point—upon the closing of the primary path from the body to the soul, vision—other *paths* unknown to us are opened, or dormant and half-awakened powers of the soul are brought into activity. ## REMARKS ON ANIMAL MAGNETISM.

Traces of animal magnetism <note>Also known as Mesmerism, this was a 18th and 19th-century theory that an invisible natural force (a "magnetic fluid") flowed through all living things and could be manipulated for healing.</note> are found as far back as deepest antiquity, in India. 

The priestly class of Brahmins in Hindustan practiced this science for several centuries, having derived its origins and rules from the Divine Service established by Brahma and from oral traditions. 

In the Vedanta of Brahma <note>The author likely refers to the *Vedas*, the oldest scriptures of Hinduism.</note> we find confirmation of this; and the teachings of Zoroaster and the *Zend-Avesta* allow us to conclude with certainty that the Persians in Asia—and through them, the Egyptian priests in their mysteries—borrowed from here the concept of magnetism and its miraculous effects. 

Baldinger, <note>Ernst Gottfried Baldinger (1738–1804) was a prominent German physician and editor of influential medical journals during the Enlightenment.</note> in his *Medical Journal* of 1787, in the thirteenth volume on page 26, writes the following regarding magnetism: 

The Jesuits, at the very beginning of their order, brought the practical teaching of these mysteries to Europe from India 

<summary>This page argues that animal magnetism is not a new invention but an ancient practice originating with the Brahmins in India, later passing through Persian and Egyptian traditions. It cites 18th-century medical scholarship and the role of the Jesuits in bringing these "mysteries" to Europe.</summary>

<keywords>animal magnetism, India, Brahmins, Brahma, Vedanta, Zoroaster, Zend-Avesta, Egyptian mysteries, Baldinger, Jesuits</keywords>

through their missionaries. It is well known that in no other monastic order are there men as learned as among them; and the immortal works of Kircher <note>Athanasius Kircher (1602–1680) was a German Jesuit polymath who published works on medicine, geology, and East Asian studies.</note> and Caspar Schott <note>Caspar Schott (1608–1666) was a Jesuit physicist and mathematician, a student of Kircher, famous for his works on "natural magic" and mechanical inventions.</note> bear witness to how long ago they were knowledgeable in the **secret sciences** <term>secret sciences: This term refers to the "occult" or hidden natural philosophies of the time, such as alchemy and early experimental physics, which were not yet part of mainstream university curricula.</term>. Even Mesmer himself, who was presented as the inventor of magnetism, owes the first foundation of his knowledge to the famous Father Hell, <note>Maximilian Hell (1720–1792) was a Jesuit astronomer and priest. He used magnets to treat illnesses, a technique that directly inspired Franz Mesmer’s later development of "animal magnetism."</note> who was a very learned man.

It seems to me quite unjust how the author of the letter published in Baldinger’s journal judges them, accusing the members of this society <note>Refers to the Society of Jesus, or the Jesuits.</note> decisively and without proof, claiming that they used these sciences only for the secret designs of their order.

Upon reading the works of Kircher, Schott, and many other famous Jesuits like them, one will see that these men had no intention of hiding even the most secret things, provided they could be useful to humanity.

Schott, in the fourth volume of his *Medical Magic*, <note>Schott’s *Magia Universalis* (Universal Magic) was a massive four-volume encyclopedia of the "miracles" of nature and art, including acoustics and medicine.</note> writes of secret remedies and speaks of the influence of the voice and of musical rhythm.

There is no doubt that the sun truly lifts earthy, glassy, and other particles into the air; we often find hair, straw, sand, wood chips, and all manner of debris trapped within hailstones <note>Early modern scientists often used the presence of debris in hail or rain as proof that the sun's heat could "evaporate" solid matter into the atmosphere.</note>.

...however, truth will always remain truth, and the prejudices of the current century are just as much prejudices as those of past ones. I know no difference between people: they are all my neighbors, all my brothers.

## I and YOU,

### A Noteworthy Chapter, belonging to the previous one.

In Nature <note>original: "Натура." In the 18th and early 19th centuries, Nature was often personified as a divine or semi-divine system of order.</note> there is so much that is great, so much that is incomprehensible to man! And yet, the solution to these riddles is closer to us than we think.

*I* and *you* — these are important words, providing many solutions to obscure ideas.

What is *I* without *you*? — A being entirely separate, without connection. Nature did not create solitary beings: her law is community.

God’s works consist in union: unhappy is that link which separates itself from the universal chain <note>The "Great Chain of Being" was a popular philosophical concept during the Enlightenment, suggesting that all existence is linked in a continuous, hierarchical order.</note>.

In Nature, everything is connected to one another, and this necessary connection constitutes the laws of preservation for the physical world.

### Physiognomy of the Soul

<margin><unclear>P</unclear></margin> There are circumstances in which a man takes falsehood for truth, and evil for good.

The face of the human spirit is very different from the face of his body. The outward appearance of the body is inherited from parents; but the appearance of the spirit comes from the inclinations which it begins to <term>shape<note>original: "изображать." Literally "to depict" or "to portray," suggesting the soul carves or paints its own features through its habitual desires and moral choices.</note></term> within itself.

The human eye can be deceived by the physical face: but the eye of the spirit is not deceived by the appearance of the spirit. The image of the spirit receives its form according to its inclinations. The closer a spirit is to pure love, the more beautiful is its <term>countenance<note>original: "зракъ." An archaic Russian term referring to one's appearance, vision, or facial expression; here it refers to the "look" of the soul.</note></term>; for the properties of love are <term>Wisdom<note>original: "Премудрость." Often associated with Sophia, the Divine Wisdom.</note></term>, Beauty, and Power.

Man exists in a twofold connection, with the corporeal and spiritual worlds; according to the first, he is called external, according to the second, internal.

The good and the true are spiritual, and proceed from love and light.

Love, from which all that is good and true flows, is twofold: Love for God, and Love for one's neighbor. It makes us <term>friends of Heaven<note>original: "друзьями Неба." A phrase common in 18th and 19th-century mystical literature, suggesting that through divine love, humans achieve a state of harmony and kinship with the celestial realm.</note></term>.

Love, from which that which is evil and false flows, is love of self and of the world: from it come all misfortunes, and from it Hell was born.

Two things constitute the life of the spirit: Love and Faith. Love constitutes the life of the will, and Faith the life of the mind. Blessed is he who dwells always in love, who does not darken in his soul the pure image of Divine beauty!

Refers to the higher, spiritual essence of a person that seeks connection with the divine and acts according to conscience.</note></term>. The more the spirit-man dominates the beast-man, the more perfect is his <term>creatureliness<note>original: "тварность." This refers to the state of being a "creature" (a created being). In this context, it suggests that a human fulfills their intended purpose as a creation of God when their spiritual side leads.</note></term>: but the more power the beast-man has over the spirit-man, the more imperfect he becomes.

<margin>|</margin>And so perfection and imperfection, good and evil, depend upon the preponderance of the spirit-man or the beast-man.

The beast-man lives in falsehood and self-love, that is, in evil: the spirit-man lives in truth and goodness, that is, in love for God and for one's neighbor.

<margin>|</margin>The relationship of the spirit-man to the <term>Divinity<note>original: "Божество."</note></term> is one of approach and likeness: while the relationship of the beast-man is one of distancing and separation.

Approach is the path to <term>bliss<note>original: "блаженство." In this tradition, bliss is not merely a feeling, but the state of eternal spiritual happiness and union with the divine.</note></term>: distancing is the path to perdition.

<margin>|</margin>The more a person becomes like the Divinity, the more necessarily perfect he is. He draws closer to truth and love, and consequently to <term>Wisdom<note>original: "Премудрость." Referring to Divine Wisdom or Sophia, the ultimate spiritual insight.</note></term>; for the knowledge of the true is the theory of Wisdom, and love is its prac- <note>The word is truncated at the page break; it is almost certainly the beginning of "practice" (практика).</note>

## ON A LONG LIFE, AND THE ART OF BECOMING YOUNG AGAIN.

If in any part of our body movement stops: then this part dies, and is no longer capable of any action.

If movement stops in the whole body: then it is often quite impossible to restore it, and then the whole body dies.

Our bodily life, says <term>Wenzel<note>Likely Karl Friedrich Wenzel (1740–1793), a German chemist and physician whose works on chemical affinity and medicine were influential in the late 18th century.</note></term>, is a product of <term>moistures<note>original: "влажностей." Referring to the "vital humors" or "radical moisture," a concept in pre-modern medicine suggesting that life is sustained by a fundamental internal fluid that gradually dries out as one ages.</note></term>,

<summary>This page concludes the attributes of Divine Wisdom regarding natural sciences and begins a new treatise on the biological mechanics of movement, aging, and the preservation of the body's "moistures."</summary>
<keywords>Wisdom of Solomon, elements, natural philosophy, botanical knowledge, Wenzel, vitality, humors, longevity, rejuvenation</keywords>

--- Page 259 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 222 regarding the nature of the body as a system of fluids and vessels. It explores the physiological paradox where the very forces that sustain life also cause the physical decay of the body.</meta>

...passing through all the appropriate vessels.

The forces by which these <term>moistures<note>original: "влажности." Here referring to bodily fluids or "humors" that sustain life by circulating through the body.</note></term> move act upon these vessels everywhere with such power that, if the particles worn away by them were not renewed, their complete destruction would necessarily follow.

Consequently, the life of an <term>animal<note>original: "животнаго." In 18th-century scientific Russian, this term refers broadly to any "animate" or living being, including humans, in a biological sense.</note></term> is itself the instrument of death.

So that the death of the animal does not occur prematurely, <term>Nature<note>original: "Натура." Frequently personified in this era as a wise, self-regulating guide or architect of the physical world.</note></term> renews what is lost through food.

But this renewal occurs, first of all, through the work of the vessels in the animal’s body, which digest the particles of food into substances appropriate to the animal’s body.

For this chemical work of the vessels, there is a need for the unhindered <term>circulation of moistures<note>original: "кругообращеніе влажностей." This reflects the medical understanding of the era, combining the discovery of blood circulation with older theories of "humoral" balance.</note></term>, their movement in accordance with the solid particles, and finally, the softness of the vessels and their capacity to give way, or expand. This balance of juices and the connection of parts constitute human health.

When this connection is broken, then disease begins; but when it is in the bo—

...body of the <term>animal<note>original: "животнаго." In the 18th-century scientific context, this term refers to any living, breathing creature, including humans, viewed as biological organisms.</note></term> is completely destroyed, then bodily death occurs.

Experiments confirm that in <term>dephlogisticated air<note>original: "воздухѣ обезгорюченномъ." From which it follows that the less the air is filled with <term>combustible substance<note>original: "горючимъ веществомъ." A reference to the humoral theory of medicine, where health was defined by the proper balance and movement of bodily fluids.</note></term> must reside in the combustible substance found in the air which animals breathe; since they live longer in air purified of combustible substance, it follows that the air we inhale is the primary cause of our diseases, old age, and premature death.

From <term>Anatomy<note>original: "Анатоміи." The study of the body's structure, which the author uses to provide physical evidence for chemical theories.</note></term> it is known that in old age the vessels <term>ossify<note>original: "костенѣютъ." To turn into bone or to harden. The author is describing the hardening of the arteries and tissues as a mechanical process of mineral accumulation.</note></term>, and that bones are of a <term>calcareous composition<note>original: "известковатаго состава." Composed of lime or calcium; the theory here is that minerals from the air or food "precipitate" into the solid parts of the body.</note></term>: consequently, this ossification is the action of the combustible substance. All <term>hot drinks<note>original: "горячіе... напитки." While this can mean temperature-hot, it was a common term for "strong spirits" or alcoholic liquors, which were considered to be full of "combustible" elements that accelerated the body's decay.</note></term> mixed with a large amount of combustible substance produce a calcareous sediment.

### Palingenesis, or Resuscitation.

By the word *Palingenesis* <note>From the Greek 'palin' (again) and 'genesis' (birth). In alchemy and early biology, this referred to the experimental "rebirth" of a plant from its ashes.</note> is understood the resuscitation of some destroyed natural body, either in its outward appearance only, or in all of its properties.

Charles Bonnet (1720–1793) was a Swiss naturalist who theorized that all living things contain "germs" that allow for their restoration or rebirth in a future state.</note></term>, **Palingenesis** is the past and future state of living beings. According to his own...

<meta>This page continues the definition of Palingenesis, following the mention of Charles Bonnet’s theories on the previous page.</meta>

meaning of this word, it is the renewal of a destroyed body, a return of it to its former state of existence.

Palingenesis is divided into supernatural and natural. The latter is divided into the Palingenesis of animals, plants, and minerals; and it is divided again into **imaginary** and **actual** Palingenesis.

**Imaginary** palingenesis can be produced through optics, Chemistry, electrical force, and magnetism. By each of the aforementioned means, I can present the resuscitation of an animal or a plant. <note>The author likely refers here to "phantasmagoria" or optical illusions—such as the use of mirrors or magic lanterns—which were often used in the 18th century to trick audiences into believing they were seeing the ghosts of plants or animals rising from their ashes.</note>

**Actual** Palingenesis is the return of a destroyed body to its former state of being, as we have said above; and it is this which is subject to so many disputes among Scholars. Those who have written about it include **Plato**, **Seneca**, <note>Thomas Erastus (1524–1583), a Swiss physician and theologian who argued against the magical claims of Paracelsus.</note>**Erastus**, <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian polymath who was a central figure in medieval medicine and philosophy.</note>**Avicenna**, <note>Ibn Rushd (1126–1198), an Andalusian philosopher who sought to reconcile Aristotle with Islam.</note>**Averroes**, <note>Likely Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a medieval German bishop and philosopher interested in alchemy.</note>**Albert**, <note>Jean Fernel (1497–1558), a French physician who was the first to describe the spinal canal.</note>**Fernel**, <note>Likely Andrea Caesalpinus (1519–1603), an Italian physician and botanist who classified plants by their fruits and seeds.</note>**Caspalin**, <note>Girolamo Cardano (1501–1576), a polymath who wrote extensively on mathematics, medicine, and the occult.</note>**Cardan**, **Cornelius Agrippa**, **Cesare Vanini**, **Campanella**, **Borel**, **Hartmann**, **Theophrastus** <note>Theophrastus von Hohenheim, known as Paracelsus (1493–1541), the father of toxicology and a believer in chemical medicine.</note>, **Maurer** in his book *Amphitheater of Universal Magic* <note>original: "Amphiteatrum magiae universae." A work by Caspar Maurer published in 1669 covering various occult and natural phenomena.</note>, **Buffon**, **Needham**, **Trembley**, and **Peyssonnel**. <note>The list ends with 18th-century naturalists like Georges-Louis Leclerc, Comte de Buffon, and Abraham Trembley, showing the transition from alchemical "resurrection" to biological study of regeneration.</note>

It is true that very many experiments regarding actual <term>Palingenesis</term> <note>From the Greek 'palin' (again) and 'genesis' (birth). In this context, it refers to the alchemical process of regenerating a plant or animal from its own ashes, often preserved in a sealed glass vessel.</note> were deceptive; but it is also true that it can indeed be produced both in plants and in animals, and that its foundation consists of a more intimate knowledge of Nature and the laws of bodies.

In 1687, <note>There is a chronological discrepancy here; Athanasius Kircher died in 1680. The author may be referring to a posthumous publication or a specific demonstration recounted by others in 1687.</note> Kircher showed the resuscitation of a rose to Queen Christina of Sweden. He received this secret from Emperor Ferdinand III, who had learned it from Archduke Maximilian; and it had been revealed to him by the famous Terenzio.

The recipe for this is found in a work printed in 1785 in Frankfurt and Leipzig under the title *Künstliche Auferweckung der Pflanzen, Menschen und Thiere aus ihrer Asche*, <note>original: "Künstliche Auferweckung der Pflanzen, Menschen und Thiere aus ihrer Asche"</note> that is: *The Artificial Resuscitation of Plants, Humans, and Beasts from their Ashes*. However, this work is incomprehensible, and it is impossible to perform experiments according to the methods prescribed therein.

Whoever solves the following questions shall find the secret of <term>Palingenesis</term>.

What is a plant? What is a flower?

What are its constituent parts?

The finest particles that make up a flower, which are invisible to our eye—do they still remain the flower?

Do they also have their own connections, form, and appearance?

How long does this form and appearance persist?

On what does it depend?

Do they change, and when, and how?

When does the flower cease to be a flower?

What is required for this cessation, for this change?

When the coarser substances <note>The author distinguishes between "coarse" (visible) matter and "subtle" (invisible or spiritual) matter, a common concept in alchemical physics.</note> change, do the subtle ones also change?

Can this change be prevented, and in what proportion will those substances then be?

he employs a magic rod <note>The Russian *волшебный прутикъ* literally means "magic rod," but here refers specifically to the dowsing rod mentioned on the previous page.</note>; and it is all the same to him from what tree or at what time it was cut, nor does he have any need for any rituals in the process. Upon entering it, the peasant felt a terrible change within himself: his pulse beat as if in a fever, and the rod struck forcefully in both those places where the bodies of the Vintner and his wife had been found. Then, emerging from the cellar, he followed the movement of the rod through all the streets that the murderers had traversed; he went out of the city, crossed the River Rhone <note>original: *рѣку Рону*</note> via the bridge, and turned right along the river, always following the movement of the rod.

Three men sent with him recounted that he sometimes discovered the tracks of all three criminals, and at other times only two. Upon arriving at the house of a gardener, he discovered that all three had been there, had sat at the table, and had drunk from one of the three bottles in the room, which the rod then struck. Two children, about nine or ten years old, actually...

No sooner had the news of this case spread throughout Lyon than many opinions appeared in confirmation and refutation of it. Everyone judged according to their own notions, which were as crooked, weak, and unfair as is usually the case with extraordinary occurrences. Despite all the evidence, people still did not believe, and so they proceeded to a new experiment. They blindfolded him so that he could not see the location or move the rod <term>прутикъ: a small branch or twig, often Y-shaped, used in dowsing to find water, minerals, or in this case, criminals.</term> himself whenever he wished, and in the presence of witnesses, they led him into the cellar; but the reaction of the rod proved to be the same as before.

# Opinions on this.

As soon as they became acquainted with the divining rod <term>волшебный прутикъ: literally "magic rod," a branch used in dowsing to locate water, minerals, or criminals.</term> and its extraordinary effects, they sought to explain its causes. Some attributed this to magnetic force; others derived it from sympathy and antipathy <note>In early modern science, "sympathy" and "antipathy" were used to describe natural attractions or repulsions between things that lacked an obvious physical connection.</note>, and the Aristotelians found in it a new confirmation of their doctrine of atoms. Neither *real qualities*, nor *substantial forms*, nor *hidden virtues* (*) <note>original Latin: "qualitates reales," "formae substantiales," "virtutes occultae." These were traditional Scholastic terms used to explain the inherent nature and powers of objects.</note> were forgotten, and with these names they both defended and refuted one and the same effect.

Nature in all her actions possesses a single mechanism—a single way of manifesting her countless wonders—and always proceeds by the simplest path, always employs the easiest means, and does nothing without intention or in vain: everything in her has its purpose.

If only people never lost sight of the investigation of Nature,

(*) *Material qualities, essential forms, hidden properties*—terms of Aristotelian Philosophy.

--- Page 274 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument from page 237, concluding the sentence that Nature does not use fundamentally different laws for "miraculous" events, but simply uses means that are hidden from the human eye.</meta>

employs subtle and extraordinary means, unlike her actions performed before our eyes.

Nature always acts in the same manner, and the entire wonder of her actions consists in this: that the tools and means she employs do not fall under our senses, as they do in her ordinary actions, where with a single glance we can observe cause, action, and consequence. For example: there is nothing wonderful <note>original: "чудеснаго" — in this historical context, "wonderful" refers to the "supernatural" or "miraculous."</note> in the fact that wood takes fire into itself and is ignited by it, because here Nature is not hidden in any way, and the entire process falls under our senses. When, on the contrary, dry warm smoke, or vapor, rising from mines, dries out and burns the plants and trees located on their surface: then this appears wonderful; only because the thin and sharp vapors are unperceivable to the senses. But is the mechanism <term>mechanism: the physical process and laws of motion that govern how one thing affects another.</term> not the same in both cases? —

This observation is worthy of mature reflection and provides the resolution to many mysteries of Nature.

<summary>The author argues that "wonders" are merely natural processes where the physical causes—such as invisible vapors—are too subtle for human senses to perceive. He asserts that the mechanical laws of cause and effect remain constant, whether the process is visible or hidden.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's explanation of how invisible "vapors" act as the medium for natural phenomena that otherwise appear miraculous, specifically addressing how bodies interact at a distance.</meta>

Bodies touch one another through the vapors that emanate from them, or through the atmosphere surrounding a body. In this lies the secret of the dowsing rod <note>original: "волшебный прутик" — literally "magic wand." In this scientific context, it refers to the divining or dowsing rod used to locate water or minerals, which the author believes reacts to physical vapors.</note>.

These emanating vapors are either a part of the essence of that from which they emerge, or a third substance <note>original: "существо" — here used in the archaic sense of an entity or "being" of a material nature.</note> that transfers force from the active part to the passive part <note>original: "страждущую" — literally "suffering," a term used in older physics and philosophy to describe the object that receives an action or force.</note>. Often they are simply the air located near the active bodies: an example of this is a bell, whose ringing travels to the eardrum of our ear. These emanating vapors, though they escape our sight, still retain the properties of the body from which they emerged. We see this in damp weather, when water particles that have evaporated into the air settle again upon marble, walls, and so forth due to the cold, or fall as dew and rain, and become water once more.

We observe the same with mercury, which, when subjected to the slightest heat, separates from all other bodies—no matter how it was mixed with them—and appears again in its original form. The air absorbs all kinds of vapors, and wi—

The vapors of a contagious disease <note>The author is likely referring to the "miasma theory," the historical belief that diseases like cholera or the plague were spread by "bad air" or noxious vapors before the germ theory of disease was established.</note> act in the same way as physical contact with an infected body. And so, there is nothing in Nature that does not possess its own signs or indicators. If one were to make the thinnest metallic needles and give them the freedom to move, like a magnetic needle, then among many boxes placed around them, they would stop at the one where metal is hidden. If one wets a sponge slightly and, hanging it by a thread over a vessel containing some liquid, sets it to swing, one can also determine what is poured inside based on how long the sponge adheres to the vessel; one only needs to repeat several experiments to confirm this.

Truth and falsehood are hidden in all things; therefore, a rational person rejects nothing but tests everything.

# Something about Witchcraft.

In olden times, there were many such people who, in the language of the common folk, were called *Evil People*, *Wicked People*, or *Sorcerers* <note>original: "Злые люди" (Zlyye lyudi), "Лихіе люди" (Likhiye lyudi), "Колдуны" (Kolduny) — these are traditional Russian terms for those suspected of practicing harmful magic.</note>, and who knew certain secret sciences, borrowed from the Arabs and Egyptians during the time of the Crusades and passed from one nation to another. Nowadays, these knowledges, fortunately for humanity, have almost been eradicated; however, in some places, especially in villages, there are people who do much harm with them, as I myself have happened to witness.

The primary forms of <term>witchcraft</term> <note>original: "колдовство" (koldovstvo) — here referring to folk magic or hexing intended to cause domestic or agricultural misfortune.</note> usually consist of the following:

1. That livestock lose their <term>cud</term> <note>original: "жвачку" — the partially digested food regurgitated from the first stomach of ruminants to be chewed again. In folk belief, a cow "losing its cud" was a common sign of a curse.</note>, eat nothing, and die from it;
2. That their urine becomes bloody;
3. That a cow, instead of milk, yields blood when milked;
4. That livestock are infested with lice;
5. That the heads and necks of chickens twist about;
6. That a horse suddenly goes lame;
7. That butter will not <term>churn</term> <note>original: "пахтается" — the process of agitating cream to make butter. Failure for butter to form was often blamed on a "spoiling" spell cast by a neighbor.</note>;

<summary>The author transitions from physical science to folk belief, attributing the origins of witchcraft to ancient "secret sciences" brought from the East and listing common agricultural hexes.</summary>
<keywords>witchcraft, sorcerers, folk magic, Crusades, agricultural curses, livestock diseases, secret sciences</keywords>

### 8. That beer will not brew.

The flippancy with which everything is rejected nowadays prevents many from paying attention to these effects; and thus, they fail to find the means to avert them. Some harm themselves through <term>superstition</term> <note>original: "суевѣріе" (sueveriye) — here implying a blind belief in magic without seeking natural causes.</note>, others through unbelief.

I will propose here remedies against this, based on the very nature of the thing: as for the methods by which these mischiefs are produced, I have judged it best not to describe them, so as not to give wicked people occasion to use them to the harm of their neighbor.

1. If livestock should lose their <term>cud</term> <note>original: "жвачка" (zhvachka) — the partially digested food that ruminants like cows chew. In folk medicine, a cow "losing its cud" was a serious sign of illness, often attributed to a hex.</note>: then remove all the feed from the manger, sweep it clean, and rub it thoroughly with salt and vinegar; then provide fresh feed. If the animal still will not eat, then sprinkle <term>malted grain</term> <note>original: "рощеный солодъ" (roshchenyy solod) — sprouted and dried grain used in brewing; here used as a digestive aid to stimulate the stomach.</note> into the feed, and all will be well.

2. Bloody urine in livestock is either genuine or counterfeit. To distinguish between them, boil the urine over a fire. If the blood is genuine, it will coagulate <note>original: "ссядется" (ssyadetsya) — to clot or curdle. This test distinguishes biological blood from dyes or chemical additives.</note>, and then <term>bloodletting</term> <note>original: "кровопусканіе" (krovopuskaniye) — the historical medical practice of withdrawing blood to cure illness.</note> and <term>enemas</term> <note>original: "клистиръ" (klistir) — a common 18th-century treatment for livestock and humans alike.</note> with <term>emollient herbs</term> <note>original: "мягчительныя травы" — plants used to soothe inflammation or soften tissues, such as marshmallow root or mallow.</note> are very useful: if, however, the blood does not coagulate, then this has been counterfeited by means of a certain

...plant which was given to the livestock as fodder. This latter case has no harmful consequences; for it is not real blood, but urine that has merely taken on the color of blood <note>Certain plants, such as madder or beets, contain pigments that can pass through an animal's system and stain the urine, creating a frightening but harmless appearance.</note>.

3. The same cause can make the milk red: but it is harmful neither to humans nor to livestock; and it will pass on its own.

4. Lice in livestock can be helped by maintaining cleanliness. The best remedy, however, is to comb out the lice, dry them in a pan over a fire, grind them into a powder, and, having mixed them with <term>wormwood water</term> <note>original: "полынная вода" (polynnaya voda) — a bitter infusion made from Artemisia absinthium, traditionally used as a repellent for insects and parasites.</note>, wash the animal with this water <note>The practice of using the pest itself in the cure is a common element of "sympathetic medicine," based on the idea that a substance can be cured by its own essence.</note>.

5. <term>Falling sickness</term> <note>original: "падучая болѣзнь" (paduchaya bolezn') — historically a term for epilepsy, but here used to describe sudden collapses or seizures in poultry.</note> in chickens occurs for two reasons: firstly, if a hen eats a grass growing on walls <note>Likely referring to wall-pellitory or certain types of moss/lichen thought to be toxic to birds.</note>, which they love very much; in such a case, one must clear the walls of this grass and give the chickens wine vinegar;—secondly, from a natural disease called <term>the worm</term> <note>original: "червякъ" (chervyak) — a traditional name for various poultry ailments, sometimes referring to gapeworms or even a perceived growth under the tongue.</note>, which is easiest to prevent by smearing the heads of young chicks with <term>wooden oil</term> <note>original: "деревянное масло" (derevyannoye maslo) — a common historical term for a low-grade, pressed vegetable or olive oil used for lamps and topical folk remedies.</note>.

6. <term>Pricking a horse</term> <note>original: "заковка" (zakovka) — a common injury where a farrier accidentally drives a nail too close to or into the sensitive "quick" of the horse's hoof during shoeing.</note> can happen in an instant; and he who does not know the remedy...

### On the Language of Animals.

Language is the expression of feelings. Animals feel and express their feelings; consequently, they also speak.

The perfection of language consists in the quality of the expression of feelings. Language alone, says **Herder** <note>Johann Gottfried von Herder (1744–1803) was a German philosopher whose 1772 "Treatise on the Origin of Language" argued that language is not a divine gift but a human invention tied to the way we think.</note>, has made man into man, by enclosing the drive of his passions within limits and establishing through words monuments for the mind.

As man is the most perfect creature on earth, his language is likewise more perfect than all others.

The language of animals is insufficient, proportionate to their purpose and needs; nevertheless, it too is a language.

Animals express feelings through sounds; these sounds change according to the quality of their passions and sensations. We hear one sound from them in joy— <note>The text cuts off here at the end of the page.</note>

Vanity, arrogance, pride, and haughtiness <note>The author lists these specific vices to contrast human social complexity with the perceived "innocence" of the natural world, a common theme in 18th-century Romantic and Enlightenment thought.</note> will never be the subjects of their conversations; they express only their desires, which are limited and not numerous. A dog will never complain that its kennel <note>original: "конура" (konura)</note> is not gilded; it will not demand that its food be served on golden platters; it will not wish to be made a commander over other dogs. It requires only food. When you are angry with it, it fawns upon you; when you leave it alone, it pines for you; you return, and it rejoices. It is an enemy to your enemies; it tells you when a stranger enters, chases after game, and reveals its location to you. To these boundaries extend the limits of its language with man.

Not all animals have a vocal language; many express themselves through movements alone. Even the dog tries to supplement its expression with body movements <note>Here the author anticipates the study of ethology, recognizing that "language" includes non-verbal cues like tail-wagging or posture.</note>...

## Theory of Animal Language.

### *Assumptions.*

Beasts do not have words in the proper sense; that is, they do not designate every individual object with a specific variation of the voice. Their expressions consist for the most part of many tones; that is, what constitutes an entire phrase for a human is often only a single sound for them <note>The author suggests that animal vocalizations are "holophrastic," meaning a single cry conveys a complex state of being or a complete thought that humans would require many words to express.</note>.

The language of beasts is the expression of their true feelings: consequently, it is very simple, proportionate to their needs—which are not numerous—and truthful; for it is the expression of true feeling. A bird does not tell another that it loves it when it does not, or that it is joyful when it is not <note>This highlights a common Enlightenment-era philosophical theme: the "innocence" of nature compared to the potential for human deception and social artifice.</note>.

Among animals, birds are the most talkative <note>original: "словохотнѣе" (slovokhotneye), meaning 'more word-loving' or verbose</note>, and the following theory serves to help us understand them.

# THEORY OF THE LANGUAGE OF ANIMALS.

## *Suppositions.*

Beasts do not have words in the strict sense; that is, they do not signify every object with a specific variation of the voice. Their expressions consist for the most part of many tones—that is, what for a human constitutes an entire phrase is often for them only a single word <note>The author suggests that animal vocalizations are "holophrastic," where one sound conveys a complex emotional state or intention that humans would require a full sentence to describe.</note>.

The language of beasts is the expression of their true feelings: consequently, it is very simple, proportionate to their needs—which are not numerous—and truthful; for it is the expression of true feeling. A bird does not tell another that it loves her when it does not love, or that it is happy when it is not happy <note>This reflects the Enlightenment-era "Noble Savage" trope applied to the animal kingdom: the idea that nature is inherently honest, unlike the perceived deceptiveness of human society.</note>.

<meta>This page continues the "Theory of Animal Language," moving from the general honesty of animal expression to the specific mechanics of vocalization, focusing on the role of repetition and specific animal examples like dogs and hens.</meta>

The language of animals is repeated as long as their feeling continues. A wounded dog yelps loudly as long as the pain is intense.

Thus, the language of animals consists of incessant repetitions. A person wishing to understand them must learn the variations of the animal voice according to their passions <note>original: "страстямъ" (strastyam) — In the natural philosophy of this era, "passions" refers to primary emotional and physical states like fear, anger, or maternal instinct rather than modern "passion."</note>: only then will he understand their language.

For example, a dog, continuing its barking at night, always barks in the same manner when it wants to say: *Beware! — A stranger has come! — He has not left yet — beware!* And in this way, it repeats incessantly until its sensation and concern are stilled.

The same applies to birds, whom I have observed very closely. Their monosyllabic and disyllabic <note>The author is attempting a linguistic analysis of birdsong, breaking down "chirps" into syllables to map them to specific meanings.</note> abrupt tones express their concern.

Observe a dog when it cares for its puppies; likewise a cat and birds—for instance, a hen, especially if she was set upon duck eggs and has hatched ducklings. The ducklings swim, while the mother, running...

...along the shore with anxiety, uttering disyllabic <note>original: "двусложныя" (dvuslozhnyya) — The author continues his linguistic analysis, categorizing bird calls by the number of "syllables" they seem to contain to map them to specific meanings.</note> sounds, which are the expression of her concern. On the other hand, quick, harmonic tones following one after another are expressions of pleasure and joy, just as, for example, the lark expresses its joy at the arrival of morning.

Quick, indistinct, hurried tones are an announcement of something good, as for example the sparrow, upon seeing food, notifies its brothers of it.

It is told of **Apollonius of Tyana** <note>A celebrated Greek philosopher and mystic from the 1st century AD. In historical lore, he was often attributed with the divine or "natural" gift of understanding the languages of animals.</note> that he once, while standing before a house and listening diligently to the sparrows, announced that he had heard from them that grain had been spilled nearby, and one of them had brought the news to the others: everyone was amazed to find that this was truly the case.

The tones of love among birds are very different from the tones of joy; they are tenderer and more harmonic: one only needs to listen to the birds mating <note>original: "понимающихся" (ponimayushchikhsya) — An archaic term referring here to birds pairing up or mating in the spring.</note> in the spring.

### ON WORDS, HERBS, AND STONES.

<margin>p</margin>
*In words, herbs, and stones* <note>original Latin: "In verbis, herbis et lapidibus" — a common medieval and Renaissance aphorism suggesting that healing and magic reside in these three categories.</note>. Within words, herbs, and stones, the Ancients used to say, great powers are hidden.

<margin>|</margin>
Some laughed at this saying, while others took it literally; both the former and the latter were mistaken.

<margin>|</margin>
Generally speaking, the Ancients were right: but we, having misunderstood them, took it in quite another way. In ancient times, due to a lack of knowledge regarding Nature <note>original: "Натуры" (Natury) — used here in the classical sense to mean the physical world and its inherent laws.</note>, superstition and folly led people astray. In truth, there is a special science concerning the miraculous powers of words, herbs, and stones, and the Wisdom of God <note>original: "Премудрость Божія" (Premudrost Bozhiya) — a theological concept referring to Divine Wisdom as a guiding force in understanding the world.</note> teaches this to those who fear Him.

<margin>|</margin>
Words have extraordinary power over the human heart when they

There are words of expression, words of soulful sensation, and words of power.

When an inspired person speaks, wonder gathers crowds around them; their feeling becomes the feeling of others, and their sensation becomes theirs.

Through words, tears flow and are dried. Whoever understands the power of words will cheer the despondent and calm the angry.

In ancient times, stones were used not only for healing but also for various miraculous actions: yet the wonders recounted of them by the Ancients were rarely confirmed by experience. In the kingdom of plants, the following is generally observed:

1. Greenery, herbs, stalks, flowers, garden vegetables and plants, field and garden fruits, shrubs, trees, and mushrooms.

2. The parts of which they consist; for example, leaves are sometimes round, sometimes oblong, oval, or sometimes heart-shaped; their surface is sometimes smooth, sometimes rough, and sometimes soft. <margin>A bracket groups this paragraph in the original manuscript.</margin>

Flowers have a root, stem, leaves, calyx <note>the protective outer envelope of a flower</note>, corolla <note>the inner circle of petals</note>, vessels, pistil, tongue <note>original: "язычекъ" (yazychek) — likely referring to a ligule or small appendage</note>, head, and stalk. <margin>A vertical line is drawn next to this paragraph.</margin>

For a tree: root, trunk, boughs, branches, offshoots, leaves, buds, blossom, fruits, and seeds.

For fruits: the sprig, skin or shell, the body <note>the fleshy part of the fruit</note>, and the kernel or seed.

For seeds: the grain, skin, kernel, and sprout.

I. Plants are divided according to taste:

1. Into **bitter** ones.

A.) For example: wormwood, chamomile, rhubarb, aloes <note>original: "сабуръ" (sabur) — the dried juice of aloe leaves, historically used as a potent medicinal bitter</note>, cinchona <note>original: "хина" (khina) — also known as Peruvian bark, the source of quinine</note>, and others, which:

a.) soothe acidity in the stomach,
b.) cleanse the blood,
c.) break up blockages <note>original: "завалы" (zavaly) — a historical medical term for obstructions in the internal organs or vessels</note>.

### Weight, measure, and number.

**The ancient opinion of the learned:**

->By weight, measure, and number God made all things <note>original Latin: "Pondere, mensura, numero Deus omnia fecit" — a famous biblical reference from the Book of Wisdom 11:20, often cited as the foundation of the scientific method and the order of the universe</note><-
->i.e.<-
->By weight, measure, and number<-
->The Creator fashioned the universe<-

In weight, measure, and number lies the connection of all things. The sciences leading to these insights are Chemistry, Mechanics, and Arithmetic. These sciences are necessary for the Investigator of Nature’s <note>original: "Натуры" (Natury) — using the Latin-derived term rather than the Russian 'Priroda,' typical of the scientific language of the era</note> mysteries.

Whoever understands the nature of constituent parts, and knows how to decompose things and join them together again by weight, measure, and number, shall perform great miracles.

For the inquiring mind, it is sufficient to know the means of dissolving <note>original: "разрѣшенія" (razresheniya) — in this context, it refers to chemical dissolution or decomposition into constituent elements</note> substances. I shall list the most well-known here.
