### Main Principles

Every body <note>original: "тѣло" (telo) — used here in the scientific sense of "matter" or "substance"</note> has its own immutable laws, by which it is what it is, and by the change of which it is itself changed.

Every body has its component parts, and every component part is also a body of its own kind.

Component particles are divided into proximate <note>the immediate building blocks of a substance</note> and remote <note>the ultimate, simplest elements of matter</note>.

<summary>This page concludes a list of chemical solvents—including vinegar, ammonia, and caustic alkalis—and their effects on various substances before introducing fundamental principles regarding the nature and composition of physical bodies.</summary>
<keywords>solvents, chemistry, vinegar, volatile alkalis, caustic alkalis, Prussian blue, phosphoric acid, matter, component parts, proximate particles</keywords>

--- Page 301 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the fundamental principles of matter, specifically focusing on the hierarchy of component parts (proximate and remote) and how their arrangement defines the properties and powers of a substance.</meta>

Proximate <note>original: "Ближайшія" — the immediate ingredients</note> parts are the first component parts of a body <note>original: "тѣло" — used here to mean a physical substance or matter</note>; and remote <note>original: "отдаленнѣйшія" — the deeper, simpler elements that make up those ingredients</note> parts are the component parts of those component parts. For example: the proximate component parts of **cinnabar** <term>cinnabar: a bright red mineral consisting of mercury sulfide, the primary ore of mercury</term> are **sulfur** and **mercury**; the remote parts, then, would be the component parts that make up the sulfur itself.

A change in the remote component parts always changes the proximate component parts of a body; but a change in the proximate parts does not always change the remote ones.

When the order of the component parts is disrupted, the body may be destroyed; but the remote component parts may suffer nothing at all from that disruption. These same remote parts, however, cannot change without the body itself being changed.

The various qualities of bodies arise from the different measure <note>original: "мѣра" — likely referring to the proportions or the geometric arrangement</note> of their composition. Every body has its own inherent <note>original: "собственно ему принадлежащія" — literally "properly belonging to it"</note> and accidental <note>original: "случайныя" — secondary or non-essential</note> powers <note>original: "силы" (sily) — in this context, the physical or chemical properties and forces</note>.

Inherent powers consist for the most part in the relationship between the most remote component parts; whereas accidental powers relate to the body’s form.

And so, with a change of the remote component parts, the inherent— <note>the text cuts off mid-word: "соб-" (собственныя)</note>

<summary>This page defines the difference between proximate and remote component parts of matter, using cinnabar as an example, and explains how these parts determine the inherent and accidental properties of a substance.</summary>
<keywords>proximate parts, remote parts, cinnabar, sulfur, mercury, inherent powers, accidental powers, composition of matter</keywords>

--- Page 302 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on how the internal composition and external form of matter determine its properties. The previous page established that "inherent powers" come from remote component parts, while "accidental powers" come from the object's form.</meta>

...inherent powers; whereas with a change of forms, the accidental <note>original: "случайныя" — in 18th-century natural philosophy, this refers to non-essential or secondary properties, similar to the Aristotelian "accidents"</note> ones are changed.

The action of attraction in an **artificial magnet** is an accidental power; for it arises from the arrangement of parts, which are nothing more than form, and do not belong to the actual composition of the body <note>original: "тѣло" — a physical substance or object</note>.

A person can change the accidental powers of a body without producing a change in its internal powers, in consideration of the body's essence. **Gold** will always remain gold, whether in the shape of a sphere or in the shape of a cube.

The stronger attracts the weaker, and the weaker attracts the lightest.

Hardness and weakness, lightness and heaviness, consist partly in the component parts of bodies, and partly in their forms.

Every body can have true <note>original: "истинную" — essential or inherent</note> hardness and accidental hardness, true weakness and accidental weakness. Thus, a **lead sphere** is accidentally weaker than a **lead cube**, and the cube is accidentally harder than the sphere; because it is easier to set the sphere in motion than the cube <note>The author here uses "weakness" and "hardness" to describe mechanical resistance and stability rather than just material density</note>.

He who learns the component parts of a body, their measure, powers, and forms: he...

<summary>This page explains the difference between inherent and accidental properties of matter, using examples like magnets, gold, and geometric shapes to show how form affects physical behavior without changing a substance's essence.</summary>
<keywords>natural philosophy, accidental powers, magnetism, gold, lead, mechanics, hardness, weakness, essence of matter</keywords>

--- Page 303 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the relationship between the knowledge of a body’s composition and the ability to manipulate its properties. The previous page ended with the statement that one who understands the measure, powers, and forms of a body's parts is empowered to act upon it.</meta>

...can also change their actions <note>original: "дѣйствія" — in this context, the physical effects, behaviors, or chemical interactions of a substance</note>. By learning why fire burns and how a body <note>original: "тѣло" — a physical object or substance</note> is consumed, I can make that which is combustible incombustible.

The action of every body depends partly on the body itself, and partly on the objects upon which it acts.

There is no action without reaction <note>original: "противодѣйствія" — literally "counter-action"; the author is applying a principle similar to Newton's Third Law to general natural philosophy</note>; in every action, there are **acting parts** and **suffering parts** <note>original: "страждующія части" — "suffering" here means "passive" or "receptive," referring to the components of matter that undergo a change or receive an influence</note>.

When the measure of suffering parts in any body exceeds the measure of the acting parts, then a body in which the acting parts exceed the suffering ones can act upon it.

The measure of action is proportional to the capacity of the suffering parts. Every action of one body upon another is a certain kind of communication of its own inherent forces. Equal forces are not communicated; however, unequal forces produce actions.

Action corresponds to the component parts. As long as it continues, there is no equilibrium of forces between the bodies acting upon one another. This equilibrium seeks to restore itself, and from that effort arises **fermentation** <note>original: "броженіе" — in 18th-century science, this referred to any internal chemical agitation, effervescence, or "seething" of matter during a reaction</note> and motion.

**Vocabulary:** action, reaction, body, suffering parts, acting parts, equilibrium, fermentation, motion.

<summary>This page defines the mechanics of physical interaction, explaining that action and motion occur when unequal forces seek to restore equilibrium. It introduces the concept of matter consisting of "acting" (active) and "suffering" (passive) parts.</summary>
<keywords>action and reaction, passive matter, active forces, equilibrium, fermentation, natural philosophy, kinetics</keywords>

--- Page 304 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of natural philosophy, specifically focusing on how forces of action and reaction lead to equilibrium or transformation. It moves from the internal struggle of material components to the foundational role of arithmetic in understanding the physical world.</meta>

Like seeks like, and equal seeks equal. The **force of similitude** <term>original: "подобобытіе" — a term referring to the ontological drive for things to achieve a shared state of being or likeness</term>, which is found in every body, battles against foreign elements until it either makes them similar to itself or expels them—and consequently, until it changes the body or transforms it into something entirely different.

When there is no possibility of becoming similar, the struggle between the competing forces continues until the particles of the body are dissolved and the dissimilar elements are separated.

Through these principles, one can explain all the wonders of **Chemistry** and **Mechanics** <note>In the 18th century, these were often categorized as branches of "natural magic," where the practitioner uses the laws of nature to produce seemingly miraculous effects</note>; from these principles, conclusions can be drawn and consequences derived.

It reveals the certain laws of things. With its help, one learns the magnitude, extension, and measure of all bodies.*

This science consists of:
**Addition**,
**Subtraction**,
**Multiplication**,
**Division**,

from a sum, or a product:
->which is divided into<-
->Arithmetic and Geometric.<-

*The science of numbers is the study of the laws of multiplicity.* <note>The "laws of multiplicity" refers to the philosophical study of how the "many" arises from the "one."</note>

The composition of numbers seems to continue to infinity; however, it is not infinite.

From a present known number, man can arrive at the discovery of a future unknown number, or a past one.

Consequently, in calculation there lies the possibility to find the future or the past through the present, and to make it present by comparison or proportion <term>original: "содержанію" — in this context, it refers to the ratio or the containment of one value within another</term>.

Numbers are nothing other than the repetitions of unity.

Unity is unchangeable; it has no parts, is multiplied only by itself, and its product is also unity.

It is the beginning and the end of all numbers; yet it itself has neither beginning nor end.

It is itself a symbol of itself, a symbol of unity and harmony <note>original: "согласія" — can also be translated as "concord" or "agreement," reflecting the mystical idea that all things are in harmony when they return to the state of being One</note>.

<meta>This page continues the numerical sequence following the definition of Unity (Number 1) on the previous page. It outlines the mystical and symbolic properties of the numbers two through eight.</meta>

The number 2 is the first number; for it is the first multiplicity. Its measure is unity, from which it originates. It is a symbol of production, of creation, the number of knowledge <note>original: "вѣдѣнія" — refers to spiritual or divine knowledge, distinct from mere factual information</note>, love, and union.

The number 3 is the first independent number and is called holy. It is a symbol of perfection and the first cubic number <note>In this mystical context, the author may be referring to the number three as the first to represent a two-dimensional surface (a triangle), or referencing its role in forming the first solid in some esoteric traditions, though mathematically a cube is the third power.</note>.

The number 4 is the number of bodies, a symbol of firmness; by it the things of the corporeal <term>original: "тѣлеснаго" — relating to physical or material bodies</term> world are measured.

The number 5 is the offspring of the first odd number joined with the first even number, or the offspring of the even and the odd <note>In Pythagorean numerology, the first "odd" number was often considered 3 (as 1 was the parent of all) and the first "even" was 2. Thus, 2 + 3 = 5.</note>. It is a symbol of justice and union.

The number 6 is the seal of the world, a symbol of perfection and contentment. It is also called the human number, and the number of work and service <note>Likely a reference to the six days of creation in Genesis.</note>.

7 is the number of human life. It is called the number of birth, formation, and being, and is a symbol of knowledge, repentance, and forgiveness, and a symbol of time.

8 is the number of fulfillment and justice; a symbol of the destruction of temporal things, the number of bliss and joy.

<meta>This page continues the numerical sequence from the previous page, concluding the symbolic definitions of the numbers nine through twelve before transitioning into a more philosophical discussion on the nature of arithmetic and "Positions."</meta>

9. is the number of Wisdom <note>original: "Премудрости" — often refers to Divine Wisdom or Sophia</note> and knowledge; a symbol of human learning.

10. is the number of the universe, the number of the whole of human life, the number of laws.

11. is a number that has no significance <note>In many mystical traditions, 11 was considered "the number of the sinner" or an "evil" number because it steps beyond the perfection of 10 but fails to reach the sacred 12, thus representing transgression or disorder.</note>.

12. is the number of perfection and grace.

Such was the primary division of numbers among the ancients, especially among the Kabbalists; and within this division is hidden great wisdom and knowledge. But the mystery of numbers suffered the same fate as other sacred mysteries <term>original: "таинства" — can refer to religious sacraments or esoteric secrets</term>: we did not understand it—and so we rejected it.

## P O S I T I O N S .

Numbers are the distinguishing marks of a multitude of identical objects. When we wish to signify a multitude of identical objects, we *count* or *calculate*. Without numbers, man cannot determine the quantity of identical objects.

The names of numbers are arbitrary, <unclear>j</unclear>ust as all words are; but the numbers themselves are—

<summary>This page concludes the mystical definitions of numbers (9-12), noting the Kabbalistic origins of these systems, and begins a new section defining numbers as essential tools for quantifying identical objects.</summary>
<keywords>numerology, Wisdom, Kabbalists, sacred mysteries, counting, arithmetic, philosophy of mathematics</keywords>

--- Page 308 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from the previous page, clarifying that while the names we give to numbers are arbitrary human inventions, the actual mathematical truths they represent are objective and found in nature.</meta>

...are found in the nature of things, and not in the human will.

The result, or product, of many numbers added together is necessary, and resided within the very essence of those numbers even before they were added.

Numbers multiply through union and give birth to others, without losing anything themselves.

For example, 5 is a number in its own right: but 3 and 2, or 4 and 1, are its parts. However, its *true* parts are units; for 5 signifies 5 units. And so, the breaking down of every number into its constituent parts will once again produce the unit <note>original: "единица" — the number one, viewed here as the indivisible building block of all mathematics.</note>.

The unit remains always a unit in its own essence: but in relation to other numbers, it changes its nature. The relationship of the first unit to the second is called 2; the relationship of one unit to two units is 3; of two to one is also 3; of two to two is 4; of three to one is also 4; and of one to three is equally 4, and so forth.

Numbers have their own immutable laws. When a unit is added to any number, that number will necessarily increase; and when a unit is taken away, it will necessarily decrease.

Arithmetic teaches these immutable laws, and for this reason, it leads us toward the true knowledge of things. Now the question follows: what can be calculated?

Answer: everything that consists of many parts; everything that possesses time, space, and measure; everything that can be divided; everything that possesses an arithmetic nature <note>original: "ариѳметическое содержаніе" — literally "arithmetic content." The author suggests that anything with a quantifiable substance is subject to these laws.</note>.

I once staged an experiment that surprised many people. To a certain lottery enthusiast <note>original: "охотнику до лоттереи" — a "hunter for the lottery," an idiomatic way of describing someone obsessed with gambling or games of chance.</note>, I said that if I so desired, I could guess which five numbers would be drawn. "How could you possibly know that?" he asked me. "I have a certain powder," I replied, "which, if I burn it as incense <note>original: "покурю" — in this context, it refers to the practice of fumigation or burning a substance to produce smoke, often associated with alchemy or stage magic.</note>, will cause the numbers to appear upon the floor of the room." 

He began to laugh at this; but that was exactly what I wanted, so that I might surprise him all the more later on. On the day the tickets were drawn, I went to him. "Why," I asked, "did you not choose to believe me then? You surely would have guessed the numbers." 

"But show me your powder," he said. 

"If you please: here it is!"

# Necessary Assumptions for Magical and Miraculous Calculations, or Computations.

The objects of calculation <note>Russian: *schislenie*—referring to the system of numbering or arithmetic</note> are—magnitude, extension, and measure.

There are two rules for magnitude—addition and subtraction.

Addition is the method of joining together many magnitudes of the same kind.

--- Page 313 ---
<meta>This page continues the formal definitions of arithmetic operations—subtraction, multiplication, and division—following the definition of addition on the previous page. The author uses these definitions to build a framework for his "mathematical magic" demonstrations.</meta>

Subtraction teaches us to determine the difference between two magnitudes: or, what is the same thing, to determine how much of one magnitude remains if the other is taken away from it.

Multiplication consists in finding the product of a magnitude of the same kind, increased in a specific manner. Multiplication is properly understood as abbreviated addition.

Division shows how many times one magnitude is contained within another magnitude of the same kind. It is abbreviated subtraction.

That which arises from the comparison of two magnitudes is called a *Ratio* <term>Russian: *Otnosheniye*—in this mathematical context, it refers to the relationship between two values</term>, which is divided into arithmetic and geometric.

An arithmetic comparison is the difference between two magnitudes, resulting from subtraction.

A geometric comparison is the difference between two magnitudes, resulting from division <note>While we usually call the result of division a "quotient," the author uses "difference" (*raznost*) here to describe the result of any comparison</note>.

The comparison between magnitudes and quantities is called a *Proportion*.

An arithmetic proportion consists of a comparison of increase <note>Referring to the constant difference between terms in a sequence, like 2, 4, 6</note>: while a geometric proportion consists of a comparison of "how much" <note>Russian: *kolikago*—an archaic term referring to the factor or multiple by which a value grows, as in 2, 4, 8</note>.

<summary>This page defines the basic operations of subtraction, multiplication, and division, characterizing them as shorthand for addition and subtraction. It also introduces the concepts of arithmetic and geometric ratios and proportions.</summary>
<keywords>subtraction, multiplication, division, ratio, proportion, arithmetic, geometry, magnitude, mathematical principles</keywords>

--- Page 314 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on mathematical relationships, moving from simple proportions to progressions, and introduces the "excellent science" of the properties of numbers, specifically focusing on divisibility rules.</meta>

When a proportion consists of more than three terms, it is then called a *Progression*.

These are the common rules of numbers. There are others, but they are not for everyone: one of the most excellent sciences is the knowledge of the properties of numbers. <note>The author suggests that while basic calculation is common, "number theory"—understanding why numbers behave the way they do—is a more refined pursuit.</note>

The particular properties of certain numbers are as follows:

1.) If, of any two numbers, one is divisible by 3, then the product of these numbers multiplied by each other is also divisible by 3.

2.) If two different numbers are divisible by the same number, then their difference, their sum, and their product are all divisible by that same number.

3.) Numbers that are divisible by 3, whether they are added together or multiplied, produce a value which is also divisible by 3.

4.) Any number multiplied by another that is divisible by 3 results in a product that is also divisible by 3.

5.) Any number multiplied by 9, or by another number divisible by 9, produces a sum <note>original: *summa*—while we would call the result of multiplication a "product" today, the author uses "sum" here to mean the total resulting value.</note> which is divisible by 9.

### ON THE KABBALAH

The Kabbalah was the secret and symbolic theology of the Jews. It was divided into *Gematria* <note>original: "Геметрія"; the practice of assigning numerical values to letters.</note>, *Notarikon* <note>original: "Нотаріаку"; a method of creating acronyms or shorthand from phrases.</note>, *Temurah* <note>original: "Темуру"; the system of substituting or permuting letters.</note>, *Merkavah* <note>original: "Меркаву"; mysticism focused on the "Chariot" or the divine throne.</note>, and *Bereshit* <note>original: "Берешитъ"; mysticism concerning the origins of the world.</note>, and it was regarded as the science of symbolic calculation.

Its main mystery consists in the fact that the Kabbalists reduced all things associated with the number 3 into a unit, and from that unit they derived all differences and progressions in <term>harmonic proportion</term> <note>The idea that mathematical ratios govern the harmony of the universe, a concept common in both musical theory and Western occultism.</note>.

This harmonic proportion is as follows:

## THE TEMPLE OF HEALTH AND THE MIRACULOUS BED
## OF DOCTOR GRAHAM.
<note>James Graham (1745–1794) was a famous Scottish doctor who opened the "Temple of Health" in London. He was best known for his "Celestial Bed," which was claimed to cure infertility and restore vigor through the use of "magnetic" and electrical currents.</note>

Human <term>fibers</term><note>In 18th-century medical theory, "fibers" were considered the fundamental building blocks of the body. Since sensual people are everywhere numerous, and in London the wealthy are also plentiful, Doctor Graham was able to grow rich from his bed and from his Temple. This is a question to which I, for my part, answer: that in Doctor Graham, one sees a particularly inventive mind and a profound knowledge of the *physiological* and *physical* sciences. As for the fact that he took advantage of human weakness and sensuality for his own enrichment, he cannot yet be blamed even for that. When has it ever happened that an inventor received a worthy reward without resorting to some kind of charlatanry? The high-born and the wealthy do not pursue reason, but sensuality; not knowledge, but amusement.

<meta>This page continues the author’s discussion of his improvements to Father Castel’s "ocular harpsichord," moving from the theoretical concept of sensory harmony to a practical description of the device's construction and the visual experience it produces.</meta>

I ordered for myself small cylindrical glasses made of glass, of equal size, half an inch in diameter; I filled them with multicolored liquids according to the **theory of colors**; I arranged these small glasses like strings in **clavichords**<term>Clavichord: A stringed keyboard instrument common in the 18th century. Unlike the later piano, it was known for its delicate, intimate sound and was often used for practice and private composition.</term>, dividing the gradients of colors just as tones are divided. Behind these small glasses, I made small copper **valves**<note>original: *klapantsy*. Small mechanical shutters or lids used to block light.</note> by which they were covered. These valves I connected with wire so that upon striking the keys, the valves would rise and the colors would be revealed. Just as a tone falls silent when the finger leaves the key, so too the color vanishes as soon as one removes the finger; for the valves, by their own weight, immediately fall and close the small glasses. From behind, I illuminated this clavichord with tall candles. The beauty of the appearing colors cannot be described; they surpass even the most precious gemstones. Likewise, it is impossible to express the pleasantness of the sensation in the eye at the various **chords of colors**.

### THEORY OF OCULAR MUSIC.

Just as musical tones must correspond to the Author's words in a **melodrama** <note>In the 18th-century context, a melodrama was a theatrical form where a spoken text was accompanied by musical interludes to emphasize the emotional content.</note>, so too must colors harmonize with the words.

Here it is:

Words: *The homeless little orphan.* <note>original: "Bezpryutnaya sirotinochka." The use of the diminutive "sirotinochka" conveys a sense of pity and tenderness.</note>
Tones: Mournful flute tones.
Colors: Olive, mixed with rose and white.

Words: *Walking through the meadows among the flowers.*
Tones: Rising, cheerful tones.
Colors: Green, mixed with violet and **pale straw-yellow** <term>Pale-straw: (original: blëdnopalevyy) A light, yellowish-beige or buff color popular in 18th-century descriptions of textiles and light.</term>.

From these experiments I moved to electrical ones. Thus, it is impossible to precisely determine another person's taste, smell, touch, and hearing, because the difference in these depends on the variation in their organization <note>In this context, "organization" refers to the specific physical and biological structure of an individual's body.</note>: and for that reason, what pleases one person does not please another; what is tasty to one is repulsive to another.

### On Sight.

By understanding the theory of sight, one can arrive at many wonderful discoveries. Based on this theory, I have made spectacles <note>original: "ochki" — eyeglasses or optical lenses.</note> that differ in almost no way from ordinary ones: but when someone puts them on, I can make any object standing before them invisible.

invisible by standing in a certain spot. I have a model for this effect: any object placed at this point disappears in an instant.

In a similar, and even simpler manner, one can show people without heads, or make it appear as though they have animal heads or deer antlers.

It is incredible what wonders can be achieved simply through the refraction of light <note>original: "prelomleniye luchey" — the bending of light as it passes through different mediums, such as glass or lenses.</note>. I have constructed a machine for myself that allows me to display small items—such as a snuffbox, coins, a card, and the like—lying on a table so vividly that they appear to truly be there. This is nothing more than the reflection or shadow <note>The author uses "shadow" (ten) here to describe what we would now call an optical projection or a "pepper's ghost" illusion.</note> of the object, yet it is so deceptive that even I, on several occasions while experimenting with my own snuffbox, have forgotten myself and reached out my hand to grasp its shadow. This experiment is quite amusing when performed with a bird. Observers believe a small bird is perched upon the table, seeing all its movements, when in truth it is merely its shadow. It is equally entertaining to perform this with a candle; people are often so misled that they attempt to light another candle from the illusory flame.

<summary>The author describes various optical illusions created through mirrors and light refraction, including making objects disappear and creating lifelike projections of animals and household items.</summary>
<keywords>optics, refraction of light, optical illusions, mirrors, snuffbox, scientific experiments</keywords>

## ON THE SENSE OF SMELL.

Through the sense of smell, one can also produce various wonderful effects, especially by noting the following principles:

Smells are actually particles of the odorous body, and therefore they rarely lose its properties.

The stronger scent of one body <note>The author uses the term "telo" (body) to refer to any physical substance or object.</note> changes the scent of another, weaker one. Thus, through it, one can change the property of the body itself.

Two bodies whose scents are of equal strength do not mix and do not change.

In the conflict <note>original: "bor'bye" — literally a "struggle" or "wrestling," personifying the interaction between competing odors.</note> between the scents of two different bodies, if neither overcomes the other, a third scent is produced, which is characteristic of neither the one nor the other body.

A body loses its scent when all the sulfurous particles <note>original: "sernyya chastitsy" — in early scientific thought, "sulfur" was often used as a technical term for the volatile or flammable essence of a substance, believed to be the primary source of its smell.</note> have escaped from it.

Oils retain scents the longest because the sulfurous particles

### ON SMELL <note>original: "O Obonyanii"</note>.

Through the sense of smell, one can also produce various wondrous effects, particularly by observing the following principles:

Smells are, in fact, tiny particles of the fragrant body itself, and for this reason, they rarely lose that body's inherent properties.

A stronger scent from one object can alter the scent of a weaker one. Thus, by means of a scent, one can even change the perceived nature of the object itself.

Two objects with scents of equal strength will neither blend nor transform one another.

When the scents of two different objects compete and neither overpowers the other, a third scent is produced—one that belongs to neither the first nor the second body.

An object loses its scent once all its sulfurous particles <note>In 18th-century natural philosophy, "sulfurous particles" (*sernyye chastitsy*) did not necessarily refer to the chemical element sulfur as we know it today, but rather to the volatile, oily, or "inflammable" essence of a substance that was believed to carry its odor and color.</note> have escaped from it.

Oils retain scents the longest because their sulfurous particles

### THEORY OF TOUCH.

Nerves are spread beneath the skin 
throughout the entire body, and are divided into 
a countless multitude of branches, 
ending finally in papillae <note>original: "borodavochkami" — literally "little warts," an 18th-century anatomical term for the dermal papillae or nerve endings that provide tactile sensitivity.</note>, 
which constitute the actual organ of touch. 
And therefore touch consists in the 
action of bodies upon the sensitive 
papillae according to the property of these papillae

which for this purpose must be moist

Short and dry papillae <note>original: "borodavochki" — literally "little warts," an 18th-century anatomical term for the dermal papillae or nerve endings that provide tactile sensitivity.</note> 
almost lack sensation, which

2. All senses are essentially touch:

3. According to the difference in the sensitive 
papillae, touch is also different.

4. The difference in the sensitive papillae 
produces the variety of 
feelings.

5. Thus, according to the difference in physical organization, feelings are also different; and therefore, what pleases one may not please another, and what is tasty to one is repulsive to another.

6. Identical sensitive papillae <note>original: "borodavochki" — the 18th-century term for dermal papillae or nerve endings.</note> produce identical sensations and feelings.

7. A change in the sensitive papillae changes the sensations and feelings.

8. The change in the sensitive papillae is either substantial or accidental.

9. Substantial, based on the nature of the structure of the particles; accidental, based on their variability.

10. This variability is either external or internal.

11. 12. Sensitive particles can receive that direction either naturally or artificially.

## EXPLANATION OF THE UNUSUAL CRAVINGS, WHIMS, AND PAROXYSMS OF PREGNANT AND HYSTERICAL WOMEN.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the dichotomy between the "brute-man" and the "spiritual man." ...seeks only sensual pleasures, while the spiritual man seeks higher spiritual sensations.

Reason elevates us toward spiritual sensations and toward knowledge; without it, we would all remain tied to the sensual—some to a greater degree, others to a lesser.

The very preservation of our body requires a moderate enjoyment of sensual pleasures. This enjoyment must align with the laws of reason and religion. But for the most part, it happens that the sensual gains the upper hand in man over the rational. The reason for this is that the majority of people do not know true pleasure and seek it in the tickling <note>The author uses the word "щекотаніе" (tickling) to describe superficial or fleeting physical stimulation, contrasting it with the profound satisfaction of the soul.</note> of the bodily senses. The cause of this lies partly in the natural corruption <note>A reference to the theological concept of Original Sin or the innate fallibility of human nature.</note> of man from his very birth, and partly in a poor upbringing, a lack of instruction, and bad examples.

Every person has their own temperament, that is, a specific structure of the nerves, such that they are more easily aroused by a particular passion, and more willingly seek a certain sensual pleasure rather than another.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of human temperament, moving from the definition of individual predispositions to explaining their origins in heredity and the moral state of parents during conception.</meta>

The main cause of this **temperamental inclination** <note>The author is referring to the specific nervous structure and passions mentioned at the end of the previous page.</note> arises largely from our parents—from the blood and humors <term>Humors: Referring to "соки" (juices/fluids). In pre-modern medicine, the balance of bodily fluids like blood and bile was believed to determine a person's health and personality.</term> which they infused into us at our conception, and from the circumstances in which the mother found herself during the first days of pregnancy.

Every person must be taught to consider the act of procreation as the most important task of humanity, to which we should be led not by mere brutish lust, but by a higher, nobler feeling. It is the duty of parents to care for the moral well-being of their children even before they are brought into the world. Our most holy religion joins people together for this task through the sacred **sacrament of marriage**, and its commandments are: moderation, prudent restraint, and purity of morals. A curse shall fall upon the soul of one who begets children in wild fury and frenzy, when his blood burns with some form of greed or vengeance, or when the stomach is ready to burst from gluttony.

Just as unhealthy parents usually give birth to children of a weak constitution...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how the moral and physical state of parents at the moment of conception determines the character and health of their offspring. It emphasizes the need for a noble state of mind during procreation.</meta>

Thus, the spiritual weaknesses <note>original: "душевныя слабости" (dushevnyya slabosti). In this period, "spiritual" or "soulful" encompassed both psychological traits and moral character.</note> of parents can poison the spiritual qualities of their children. This is why biological brothers may have different dispositions, and why good parents may have wicked children—especially if their upbringing and good examples fail to correct them. Even the best of men has hours in which he is more subject to the influence of passions. In such moments, when anger or vengeance, or any other ignoble passion infects your humors <term>Humors: Referring to "соки" (juices/fluids). In pre-modern medicine, the balance of bodily fluids like blood and bile was believed to determine a person's health and personality.</term>, out of love for humanity, beware of enjoying the embraces of love. 

But when your heart is filled with noble sentiments, when you have done something useful for humanity, when your conscience feels the greatness of your soul: then let the Genius of Humanity <note>A personification of the guiding spirit or collective moral conscience of mankind, common in Enlightenment-era literature.</note> lead you into the embraces of your gentle spouse, and Heaven shall reward you with worthy children.

Since the sensitivity of our nerves begins from the very moment of our conception, the state of the infant depends greatly on the circumstances in which the mother finds herself, especially during the first days of her pregnancy.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the prenatal influence of a mother's state on her child. It specifically addresses how anatomical development in the womb is influenced by external impressions, explaining the physical and psychological origins of birthmarks and peculiar compulsions.</meta>

From anatomical experiments we know that before the embryo is formed, the mass of the future human body resembles a fabric made of the finest small fibers <note>original: "жилочекъ" (zhilochek). While literally "little veins," in this historical medical context it refers to the delicate structural fibers or nerves believed to form the body's foundation.</note>. Such delicate nerves are susceptible to every impression; thus, it is the mother's duty to care for both the future moral and physical state of her child. Every image produces a certain impression upon the nerves; this impression spreads through their entire tender fabric from which the infant is formed. This is the origin of birthmarks <note>The text refers to the theory of "maternal impression," a long-held historical belief that a mother's thoughts, fears, or sights could physically manifest as marks or deformities on the unborn child.</note>.

A person's organization <term>Organization: Referring to the "организація" (organizatsiya), the fundamental structural makeup and constitutional health of the human body's systems.</term> is often corrupted at the very moment of conception, and from this arise strange whims and idiosyncrasies in a person. Mr. Benzel relates that his friend, Doctor Halovki, despite all his efforts to overcome it, could not touch velvet; whenever he did, he would fall into a sort of faint and into weakness. Conversely, I knew a man who could not spend the night with his wife without first rubbing the fingers of both his hands against velvet.

I explain both these phenomena in the following way. Velvet is smooth, but upon feel—

The **insane** are easy to deceive, for they are incapable of reasoning: one must only avoid arguing with them directly. About two years ago, a similar **adventure** happened to me.

One morning, I was told that some unknown man wanted to see me and declare something important. I ordered him to be let in. <meta>This page continues the discussion of mental health and social behavior, citing the Swiss physician Zimmermann. It concludes the section on the behavior of women before transitioning into a more theoretical philosophical inquiry into the nature of human passions and their role in intellectual discovery.</meta>

Jealous women, he says, are the most wicked of all and resemble devils. But let us turn to pregnant and <term>hysterical <note>In the 18th century, "hysteria" was a common medical diagnosis for a wide range of emotional and physical symptoms in women, then believed to be caused by disturbances of the uterus (Greek: *hystera*).</note></term> women, and conclude with the suggestion of certain remedies for their ailments.

Women subject to unusual cravings <note>original: "прихотямъ" (prikhotyam) — referring to the specific food cravings or behavioral whims traditionally associated with pregnancy.</note> should refrain from all strong drinks; on the contrary, they should consume drinks that cool the blood and make use of fresh air and cold baths. During pregnancy, one should bleed them <note>Phlebotomy, or bloodletting, was a standard medical treatment of the era, intended to balance the body's internal pressures.</note> more often, but only in small amounts, so as to merely weaken the force of the blood which oppresses the nerves. It is also good to bind their bellies so that the weight does not overly trouble the nerves: generally, however, moral and physical restraint is the best medicine.

### ON EXTRAORDINARY SENSATIONS AND FEELINGS.

Sensations and feelings, as we have proven, depend for the most part on the organization of the body.

No one can know the feelings of another without first learning the structure of their nerves, the circulation of their juices <term>juices: likely referring to "humors" or bodily fluids, as per the medical theories of the time</term>, and the rapid or slow impression of things upon their fibers <term>fibers: an 18th-century term for the basic structural threads of the nervous system and muscles</term>.

There are feelings and sensations that go beyond the ordinary. There are people who possess an extraordinary irritability <note>original: "раздражаемость"—meaning sensitivity or responsiveness to stimuli</note> in their nerves, and an imagination that can astonish. I know a man who feels so strongly, so vividly, that when he is occupied with something, nothing exists for him except that ob-

## ON MUSIC, AND THE POWER OF HARMONY OVER THE SOUL.

The rapid mobility of the brain is the cause of a sharp, penetrating intellect <term>intellect [razum]: the faculty of reason and understanding</term>. Pythagoras <note>The Greek philosopher Pythagoras (6th century BCE) is famously credited with discovering the mathematical foundations of musical intervals and using music for "soul-purification" and healing.</note> used to excite this mobility in his disciples in the mornings through music; and indeed, it seems to be based on the brain's great capacity for receiving impressions; for in the foolish, by the nature of their nerves, the mobility of the brain is very slight, and in the simple-minded it is almost entirely absent.

Tones, penetrating the human soul through the ear, bring the finest fibers <term>fibers [fibry]: in the 18th-century view of physiology, these were the microscopic components of the nerves thought to vibrate like strings when stimulated by the senses</term> into a quiet shivering. It is known that every passion <note>Original: *strast’*. This refers to a powerful emotion or movement of the spirit that "acts" upon the physical body.</note> manifests its action through a certain tension of the fibers and nerves, and that the human soul—

From this, it follows that music is the most effective means for producing this change. But for this, it is required:

1. That the Music corresponds to the passion <note>Original: *strast’*. In 18th-century thought, a passion was a powerful emotion that physically affected the body's internal balance.</note> of that person;
2. That the tones tune the nerves that have been untuned by passion;
3. That this tuning does not happen all at once; rather, the transition from anger to tranquility, or from despondency to cheerfulness, should proceed gradually through the degrees of the tones.

To achieve perfection in this science, psychological knowledge <term>psychological knowledge [psikhologicheskiia poznaniia]: in this era, "psychology" referred specifically to the study of the soul and its interaction with the physical mechanics of the body</term> is necessary.

A musician must know the structure of the human nerves; they must observe the various states of the nerves during different passions; they must be able to categorize passions by their degrees; they must know the degree of nerve tension according to the nature of a person’s temperament <term>temperament [temperament]: the historical theory that a person’s physical and mental character was determined by the balance of bodily fluids or "humors"</term>; they must establish a proportion between the musical tones and the tension of the nerves; and finally, they must derive conclusions and consequences from this knowledge.

# ON DECEIVERS AND THE DECEIVED, ON CHARLATANS AND WICKED PEOPLE.

There are many people possessing rare natural and wondrous knowledge, and performing miracles with it; because the causes of these wonders are unknown to the majority of people.

If they present their art to people for amusement—to show their agility, the power of mechanics, the effects of optics, and other physical phenomena—then they are not harmful; indeed, they sharpen the mind <note>Original: *razum*—intellect or reasoning capacity.</note> and lead to further discoveries. As long as they remain within these limits, they deserve respect as good physicists and mechanicians—such as, for example, the famous Comus <note>Nicholas-Philippe Ledru (1731–1807), known as "Comus," a famous Parisian stage magician who used scientific principles for his illusions.</note> in Paris, as well as Philadelphia <note>Jacob Philadelphia (1735–1795), an American-born magician and physicist who toured European courts.</note>, Pinetti <note>Giuseppe Pinetti (1750–1800), an Italian illusionist known for his "natural magic" and complex mechanical devices.</note>, and others.

But when these people step beyond these boundaries and, to give themselves more importance, combine their experiments with sacred rites and sprea— <note>The text cuts off mid-word (likely "spreading" or "spreading lies") at the end of the page.</note>

These lands were famous for the sciences even before Egypt, and it seems the Egyptians themselves admitted that they received their sciences from others; for they sent almost all the Greeks who came to them to learn wisdom further East for more advanced knowledge, which they themselves could not provide.

---

Indeed, many of these writers often erred and merely copied from ancient authors without their own experience and without understanding them; because of this, caution and selection are necessary so as not to fall into error. I also deem it necessary to note that some inventions which we attribute to current times were known completely, or in part, even to the ancients. For example: **inflammable air** and **dephlogisticated air** <term>Hydrogen and oxygen</term>, the discovery of which we have boasted, were known even in antiquity. In the works of Gaspar Schott, we find proposed methods for an **air balloon**; and in the *Gabalistic books* <note>Referring to *Le Comte de Gabalis* (1670), a famous satirical work on the occult and elemental spirits.</note> there is a description of the effects of dephlogisticated air; and Kircher knew of the effects of **magnetism**.

<summary>This page lists historical and contemporary authorities on natural magic and illusion, while arguing that modern "discoveries" like oxygen and air balloons were actually known to ancient and Renaissance scholars.</summary>

Deschamps.
Pereire. <note>Jacob Rodrigues Pereira (1715–1780), the first teacher of deaf-mutes in France.</note>

### On Antipathy and Sympathy:
<note>In early modern science, "Antipathy" and "Sympathy" were the hidden forces used to explain natural attractions and repulsions, such as magnetism or the effects of herbal medicine.</note>

Schott in his *Magic*, volume 4.
Kenelm Digby. <note>Sir Kenelm Digby (1603–1665) was a British courtier and philosopher famous for his "powder of sympathy," which was supposed to heal wounds by being applied to the weapon that caused them.</note>
Wagenseil, *Six Dissertations*, Altdorf, 1686. <note>Johann Christoph Wagenseil (1633–1705) was a German Hebraist and jurist.</note>
Mizaldus — *A Little Forest of Some Memorable Secrets of Nature*, of various things... <note>original Latin: "memorabilium aliquot naturae arcanorum silvula, rerum va-". Antoine Mizauld (1510–1578) was a French physician and astrologer known for his "secrets" of nature.</note>

<summary>This page lists major 17th and 18th-century authorities on two distinct topics: the education of the deaf and the natural philosophy of "sympathetic" and "antipathetic" forces. It highlights a transition from viewing these subjects as semi-magical to treating them as areas of scientific and humanitarian study.</summary>
<keywords>deaf education, sign language, Juan Pablo Bonet, Abbé de l'Épée, natural magic, sympathy and antipathy, Kenelm Digby, Gaspar Schott, bibliography</keywords>

--- Page 403 ---
<meta>This page continues a bibliographic list of works concerning natural philosophy and early science. The opening text completes a citation for a work on "sympathy and antipathy" (natural attractions and repulsions) discussed at the end of page 364.</meta>

...encompassing the sympathies and antipathies of various things, or natural harmonies and discords, in two small books <note>original Latin: "variarum sympathias et antipathias, seu naturales concordias et discordias libellis duobus complectens."</note>. Frankfurt, 1592, 1613.

Erasmus, *On the various sympathy and antipathy of all kinds of things* <note>original Latin: "de varia rerum omnis generis sympathia et antipathia."</note>.

The most famous and rarest book is the work of Adam Zaluziansky of Zaluzian, a Physician <note>original Latin: "Medici." Adam Zalužanský ze Zalužan (c. 1555–1613) was a Bohemian botanist who was one of the first to suggest the sexuality of plants.</note>, published in Prague in 1592 and then printed a second time in Nuremberg under the title: *Three Books on the Method of Herbs regarding the sympathy of plants* <note>original Latin: "Methodi herbaria libri tres de sympathia plantarum."</note>. Voigt, in his *Literary Acts of Bohemia and Moravia* <note>original Latin: "acta Bohemiae et Moraviae litterariae." Adauctus Voigt was an 18th-century historian and pioneer of Bohemian bibliography.</note>, mentions it.

### On swimming through the air, the following have written:
<note>This section catalogs early theories on aeronautics and the buoyancy of objects in the atmosphere.</note>

Father Lana. <note>Francesco Lana de Terzi (1631–1687) was an Italian Jesuit priest and aeronautical pioneer who proposed a vacuum airship.</note>

Albert of Saxony. <note>A 14th-century German philosopher known for his work on logic and physics; he speculated on the nature of the atmosphere.</note>

Mendoza. <note>Likely Francisco de Mendoza (1573–1626), a Jesuit who discussed natural wonders and physics.</note>

Schott, in the fifth book of his *Hydrostatic Magic* <note>original Latin: "Magia hydrostatica." Gaspar Schott (1608–1666) was a student of Athanasius Kircher and wrote extensively on mechanical and "magical" physics.</note>.

This same author explains the reasons for the floating of clouds in the air. He speaks of floating islands, about which Dausquius also wrote in *Earth and Water, or Fluctuating Lands* <note>original Latin: "Terra et aqua, seu terrae fluctuantes." Claudius Dausquius (1566–1644) was a French Jesuit scholar.</note>. Tournai, 1633.

To this category also belong noteworthy works

Georg Agricola,
Jacques Besson,
Agostino Ramelli. <note>These three authors were the most influential "machine book" writers of the Renaissance, documenting advancements in mining, hydraulics, and mechanical engineering.</note>

For the invention of "wind-makers" <note>original: вѣтрениковъ</note>, or ventilators, we are indebted to the aforementioned Father Schott. In the 7th book of the 3rd part of his *Natural Magic* <note>original Latin: "Magiae naturalis." Kaspar Schott (1608–1666) was a Jesuit scientist known for popularizing mechanical inventions.</note>, he writes about a wind-maker used for cooling a bedroom.

### *Registry of journals and other periodical works in which secret philosophical sciences and Magic are discussed.*

*Physico-Medical Acts* <note>original Latin: "Acta physico-medica"</note>, 1727–1754. Ten volumes.
*New Acts* <note>original Latin: "Nova acta"</note> from 1757 to 1778. Six volumes.
*Acts of the Erudite* <note>original Latin: "Acta eruditorum"</note>, Leipzig, 1685. Two volumes. <note>This was the first scientific journal of the German-speaking world, founded in 1682.</note>
*Commentaries on developments in natural science and medicine* <note>original Latin: "Commentarii de rebus in scientia naturali et medicina gestis"</note>. Leipzig, 1752–1781. 24 parts in octavo <note>A book size where the printed sheet is folded three times to create eight leaves.</note> and 3 volumes of supplements.
*Miscellany of the Academy of the Curious in Nature* <note>original Latin: "Academiae naturae curiosae miscellanea"</note>, or the *Medical-Physical Curiosities of the Academy of the Curious in Nature* <note>original Latin: "miscellanea curiosa medico-physica Academiae naturae curiosorum"</note>, or the *Acts of the Centuries and Decades of the Medical-Physical Curiosities* <note>original Latin: "Ephemeridum medico-physicarum curiosarum decuriae centuriae acta"</note>, in octavo. <note>These various titles refer to the prestigious journals of the Leopoldina, the oldest German-speaking academy of sciences.</note>

Honoré Niquet. <note>Honoré Niquet (1585–1667), a Jesuit scholar who wrote on history and theology.</note>
Juan Eusebio Nieremberg. <note>Juan Eusebio Nieremberg (1595–1658), a Spanish Jesuit natural historian and mystic writer.</note>
Lazare Nuguet. <note>Lazare Nuguet (active 1700s), a French mathematician.</note>

### O.

Olivier, member of the London Scientific Society. <note>original: "членъ Лондонскаго ученнаго общества." This refers to the Royal Society of London.</note>

### P.

Ambroise Paré. <note>Ambroise Paré (c. 1510–1590), a French royal surgeon considered one of the fathers of surgery and forensic pathology.</note>
Georg Paschius. <note>Georg Pasch (1661–1707), a German philosopher and professor who wrote on the progress of inventions.</note>
Pausanias. <note>Pausanias (c. 110 – 180 AD), the Greek traveler and geographer who wrote a detailed account of ancient Greece.</note>
Pierre Pais. <note>Pierre Pais (1564–1622), a Spanish Jesuit missionary who traveled to Ethiopia.</note>
Magnus Pegel. <note>Magnus Pegel (1547–1619), a German physician and mathematician interested in early flight and optics.</note>
Jean-Charles Perrinet d'Orval. <note>Jean-Charles Perrinet d'Orval (c. 1707–1780), a French author of several important works on pyrotechnics and fireworks.</note>
Jean Pierquin. <note>Jean Pierquin (1672–1742), a French doctor and author who wrote on magic and medicine.</note>
Giuseppe Pinetti. <note>Giuseppe Pinetti (1750–1800), a famous Italian stage magician and illusionist who performed for royalty.</note>
Alexandre Guy Pingré. <note>Alexandre Guy Pingré (1711–1796), a French naval astronomer and librarian.</note>
Johann Pistorius. <note>Johann Pistorius (1546–1608), a German physician and historian who compiled large collections of historical sources.</note>
Pliny. <note>Pliny the Elder (23–79 AD), the Roman author of the massive encyclopedia *Natural History*.</note>
Plutarch. <note>Plutarch (c. 46 – 119 AD), the Greek biographer and essayist known for his *Parallel Lives*.</note>
Giambattista della Porta. <note>Giambattista della Porta (1535–1615), an Italian scholar and polymath known for his work *Natural Magic*.</note>

### R.

Jean-Philippe Rameau. <note>Jean-Philippe Rameau (1683–1764), one of the most important French composers and music theorists of the Baroque era.</note>
Agostino Ramelli. <note>Agostino Ramelli (1531–1608), an Italian engineer who designed the famous "bookwheel" and other complex machines.</note>
Charles Aloysius Ramsay. <note>Charles Aloysius Ramsay (active 17th century), a Scottish scholar who lived in France and popularized a system of shorthand.</note>

René Antoine Ferchault de Réaumur. <note>René Antoine Ferchault de Réaumur (1683–1757), a French polymath who contributed to many fields, especially entomology, and developed the Réaumur temperature scale.</note>
Anton Maria Schyrleus of Rheita. <note>Anton Maria Schyrleus of Rheita (1604–1660), an astronomer and optician who improved the telescope.</note>
Claude Richard. <note>Claude Richard (1589–1664), a French Jesuit mathematician.</note>
Paolo Riccio. <note>Paolo Riccio (died 1541), a converted Jewish physician, philosopher, and alchemist.</note>
David Rivault de Fleurance. <note>David Rivault de Fleurance (1571–1616), a French mathematician and tutor to King Louis XIII.</note>
Cosmas Rossellius. <note>Cosmas Rossellius (16th century), a Dominican monk who wrote influential works on mnemonics and the art of memory.</note>

### S.

Charles de Saint-Évremond. <note>Charles de Saint-Évremond (1613–1703), a French soldier, epicurean, and essayist.</note>
François Poullain de Sainte-Foix. <note>Germain-François Poullain de Saint-Foix (1698–1776), a French writer and historian.</note>
Charles-Irénée Castel de Saint-Pierre. <note>Charles-Irénée Castel de Saint-Pierre (1658–1743), a French writer and diplomat known for his early proposals for an international organization to maintain peace.</note>
Pierre de Saint-Romuald. <note>Pierre de Saint-Romuald (1603–1667), a French historian and chronologist.</note>
Ippolito Salviani. <note>Ippolito Salviani (1514–1572), an Italian physician and naturalist famous for his studies of fish.</note>
Pietro Francesco Scarabelli. <note>Pietro Francesco Scarabelli (17th century), an Italian naturalist who cataloged the famous Settala museum.</note>
Thomas Shelton. <note>Thomas Shelton (fl. 1612–1620), the first English translator of Cervantes' *Don Quixote*.</note>
Wilhelm Schröter. <note>Wilhelm Schröter (1632–1689), a German jurist and legal scholar.</note>
Daniel Schwenter. <note>Daniel Schwenter (1585–1636), a German mathematician, inventor, and specialist in Semitic languages.</note>
Johann Michael Schwimmer. <note>Johann Michael Schwimmer (1629–1712), a German educator and polymath.</note>
John Duns Scotus. <note>John Duns Scotus (c. 1266–1308), one of the most important philosopher-theologians of the High Middle Ages.</note>
Achille-Pierre Dionis du Séjour. <note>Achille-Pierre Dionis du Séjour (1734–1794), a French astronomer and mathematician.</note>
Manfredo Settala. <note>Manfredo Settala (1600–1680), an Italian scientist and famous collector of curiosities in Milan.</note>
Sebastiano Serlio. <note>Sebastiano Serlio (1475–1554), an Italian Mannerist architect and influential theorist.</note>
Kazimierz Siemienowicz. <note>Kazimierz Siemienowicz (c. 1600–1651), a general of artillery and pioneer of rocketry.</note>
George Sinclair. <note>George Sinclair (died 1696), a Scottish engineer and mathematician who wrote on hydrostatics and coal mining.</note>
Nathaniel Southwell. <note>Nathaniel Southwell (1598–1676), a Jesuit historian and bibliographer.</note>
Famiano Strada. <note>Famiano Strada (1572–1649), a Jesuit historian best known for his history of the wars in the Netherlands.</note>

### T.

Gaspare Tagliacozzi. <note>Gaspare Tagliacozzi (1545–1599), an Italian surgeon and pioneer in plastic and reconstructive surgery.</note>

### U.

Diego Ufano. <note>Diego Ufano (16th–17th century), a Spanish military engineer and author of a major treatise on artillery.</note>

### V.

Philippe Vayringe. <note>Philippe Vayringe (1684–1746), a French clockmaker and engineer for the Duke of Lorraine.</note>
Godefroy Wendelin. <note>Godefroy Wendelin (1580–1667), a Flemish astronomer who observed the transit of Venus.</note>
Chevalier de Verney. <note>Likely referring to Guichard Joseph Duverney (1648–1730), a pioneering French anatomist.</note>
Jean Pèlerin, or Pelegrinus. <note>original: "или" (Russian for "or"). Jean Pèlerin (c. 1445–1524), known as Viator, was the author of the first printed work on perspective.</note>
Blaise de Vigenère. <note>Blaise de Vigenère (1523–1596), a French diplomat and cryptographer, namesake of the Vigenère cipher.</note>
Villette, father and son. <note>Famous French makers of large parabolic burning mirrors in the 17th century.</note>
Jean-Baptiste Gaspard d'Ansse de Villoison. <note>Jean-Baptiste Gaspard d'Ansse de Villoison (1750–1805), a noted French classical scholar.</note>
Wonders of Virgil. <note>original: "Merveilles de Virgile." Refers to the medieval legends portraying the Roman poet Virgil as a powerful magician.</note>
Johann Vogt. <note>Johann Vogt (1695–1764), a German theologian and bibliographer known for his catalog of rare books.</note>
Adauctus Voigt. <note>Adauctus Voigt (1733–1787), a Bohemian Piarist monk and historian.</note>

### W.

Luke Wadding. <note>Luke Wadding (1588–1657), an Irish Franciscan friar and historian.</note>
Johann Christoph Wagenseil. <note>Johann Christoph Wagenseil (1633–1705), a German Hebraist and jurist.</note>
Georg Wallin. <note>Georg Wallin the Younger (1686–1760), a Swedish bishop, traveler, and orientalist.</note>
John Wallis. <note>John Wallis (1616–1703), an English mathematician and clergyman credited with introducing the symbol for infinity.</note>
Adam Wybe. <note>Adam Wybe (1584–1653), an engineer and inventor based in Gdańsk, known for building the first cable car.</note>
Johannes Wiesel. <note>Johannes Wiesel (1583–1662), a prominent German optician and maker of telescopes and microscopes.</note>

Henning Witten. <note>Henning Witte (1634–1696), a German biographer and historian of literature known for his extensive collections of biographies of European scholars.</note>

Z.
Nicol. Zabaglia. <note>Nicola Zabaglia (1664–1750), an Italian engineer and master builder at the Vatican, celebrated for his innovative mechanical designs for scaffolding and lifting heavy objects.</note>

Zoroaster. <note>The ancient Persian prophet; in the context of 18th and 19th-century scholarship, he was often cited as a legendary source of ancient astronomical and magical knowledge.</note>
